AMD and Generative Bionics Discuss Advances in Physical AI and Robotics

AMD(681.0K)·16:51·656 views·2026-10-20
Daniele PucciCEO, Generative Bionics Salil RajeSVP and GM, Adaptive and Embedded Computing Group at AMD
TL;DRPhysical AI is emerging as a critical computing evolution that demands deterministic, low-latency, and safety-critical execution at the edge, supported by newly announced developer platforms like AMD's Kria AI robotics kit and Ryzen AI Embedded X100.
Physical Constraints & Real-World Interaction: Salil Raje notes that unlike cloud AI—which focuses primarily on throughput and relies on single-architecture GPUs—physical AI operates under strict physics, power, and safety constraints. System-Wide Complexity: Physical AI...

TL;DR

Physical AI is emerging as a critical computing evolution that demands deterministic, low-latency, and safety-critical execution at the edge, supported by newly announced developer platforms like AMD's Kria AI robotics kit and Ryzen AI Embedded X100.

Physical AI vs. Cloud AI: Architectural Demands

  • Physical Constraints & Real-World Interaction: Salil Raje notes that unlike cloud AI—which focuses primarily on throughput and relies on single-architecture GPUs—physical AI operates under strict physics, power, and safety constraints.
  • System-Wide Complexity: Physical AI requires a distributed, heterogeneous architecture spanning sensors, control loops, inference, and planning.
  • Continuous Heritage: AMD brings nearly two decades of industrial automation and robotics experience, including FPGAs, adaptive SOCs, safety, security, and sensor fusion, which serve as the foundational layer for AI workloads.

Humanoid Robotics Challenges and Edge Processing

  • Holistic Design Stack: Daniele Pucci emphasizes that developing humanoid robots like "Gene" requires simultaneously optimizing mechanics, control, electronics, and AI around environmental constraints like table heights and physical dimensions.
  • Advanced Edge Sensing: Gene utilizes distributed sensing across synthetic skin that perceives touch, proximity, and temperature alongside distributed edge cameras.
  • Edge-to-Cloud Integration: High-bandwidth sensor data must be processed at the edge to enable rationalized decision-making, with AMD providing a unified compute stack ranging from edge processing to cloud infrastructure.

Hardware Innovation: Kria AI Robotics Developer Kit & Ryzen AI Embedded X100

  • Ryzen AI Embedded X100 Architecture: Salil Raje details the chip's integrated IP portfolio:
    • 16 Zen 5 CPU cores
    • 40 RDNA 3.5 compute units
    • On-chip NPUs under unified memory
  • Hardware Form Factors: The X100 is packaged into a heat-shielded System-on-Module (SOM) that fits into carrier boards equipped with sensor fusion capabilities.
  • Developer Ecosystem: The Kria AI box combines an FPGA carrier board, the X100 SOM, and a completely open-source software stack to accelerate developer productivity.

Safety, Control Loops, and System-Level Architecture

  • Redefined Safety Standards: Daniele Pucci points out that safety in human-robot integration goes beyond standard braking; emergencies require controlled responses (such as managing a robot's fall around children rather than a dead-stop).
  • Deterministic Timing: Salil Raje explains that safety depends on system-wide architecture where control loops execute deterministically within fixed timeframes, passing data efficiently from edge endpoints through the robot's "spine" to its "brain."

Ecosystem Openness and Customization

  • Application-Specific Robots: Daniele Pucci states that a single generalized humanoid design will not suffice for diverse use cases like industrial welding versus elderly healthcare; platforms must generate application-specific robots to meet customer production KPIs.
  • Open Source Strategy: AMD supports the ecosystem by open-sourcing its embedded software stack (including ROCm for robotics) and hardware schematics for SOMs and developer platforms to foster community collaboration.

Notable Quotes

*"In the physical world, AI has to make sure that it's deterministic, it's low latency... In AI in the physical world has to worry about safety, security as well, right? And it also has to, you know, account for power, and power is a physics constraint in the physical world."* — Salil Raje
*"When you try to make a humanoid robot that interacts with human beings, really the level of safety has to be brought to another another threshold, another level... break is not safety. Okay? In the in the case of humanoid robotics. So you cannot simply stop, otherwise you may fall."* — Daniele Pucci
*"Physical AI humanoid robotics is not a sprint, is a is a marathon. So it's a long journey and in order to be effective in the long journey, you have to find the right partners that somehow want to build with you."* — Daniele Pucci
Good morning everyone, and I'm excited to be here with Salil Raje, senior vice president and general manager of AMD's embedded computing group, and Daniele Pucci, CEO of Generative Bionics. Today, we're going to talk a little bit about physical AI, a very, very exciting era for us. We're seeing all the robots rocking around the show floor here today, and we're going to dive in a little bit deeper and go into some details on physical AI. So, Salil, we've seen tremendous evolution in AI models, robotics, and computing, and today we're going to talk a little bit about all those interesting technologies. Can you tell us why you've described physical AI as the next major evolution in computing? Well, first of all, it's super exciting to be talking about physical AI. A lot of the discussion and talk has been about AI in the cloud and infrastructure, but AI truly is expanding from the cloud to the physical world. Physical AI is all about interacting with the real world and with all of us, and you know, it's quite different from the discussions we have in the cloud, right? In the physical world, AI has to make sure that it's deterministic and low latency. In the cloud, we talk about throughput, but AI in the physical world has to worry about safety and security as well. It also has to account for power, and power is a physics constraint in the physical world. It is a huge inflection point for us as AI moves into the physical world. It's not a single market, it's really a category with lots of applications appearing in the physical AI world, like autonomous driving, industrial automation, drones, and humanoid robots. So the computing in the physical world is quite different from what you see in the cloud. In the cloud, you're mostly worried about throughput, and a single compute architecture such as a GPU can handle AI in the cloud. But in the physical world, you have to think through the entire system. The entire architecture of the system has to be considered and changed. It is very distributed and very heterogeneous, which is why there's a difference between what you see in terms of compute architecture between the cloud and the physical world. We've got Gene, your humanoid robot, Daniele, out on the stage, and we can literally hear him walking around out there. Maybe you can talk a little bit about some of the challenges that you've had to overcome, some of the things that Salil mentioned, and how those apply directly to the development of Gene that you've been working on. Yeah, when you try to develop a humanoid robot that acts in the physical environment, you have to take into account not only the constraints of, let's say, the possible cognitive interaction of the entity that you're trying to develop, but also the physical constraints that dictate how the humanoid robot has to look and its properties in terms of size, weight, and degrees of freedom. So one of the challenges that we have been experiencing for designing a new humanoid robot with physical AI in it has been considering the entire stack at the same time: mechanics, control, electronics, and then AI. That stack has to be optimized for the environment where the humanoid robot has to act. Just to make examples, if the humanoid robot has to behave in front of a table which is a certain height, then you cannot not consider the environment while you design the humanoid robot. Finding the optimal as a whole is one of the challenges that you have in order to build something that is really effective for the customers, because this is what matters at the end. So Salil, obviously with the technology that's being deployed today, there's been an inflection point in terms of what's happening in the industry. Can you maybe talk a little bit about our heritage from AMD and our heritage in robotics, and then what you see as really the driving force here for this next generation of autonomous robotics that's upon us right now? Yeah, absolutely. We at AMD have been in the robotics space and the industrial automation space for almost two decades. So in some ways, this is not new, some of these applications for us. We have been working in this space on the control side and the sensing side, and we have applications from FPGAs and adaptive SOC products that have been deployed for many, many years. So we have the DNA in this space; we have worked on safety, security, deterministic compute, and the sensing side. Sensor fusion is one of our core strengths. So none of that goes away, actually, with this new physical AI world. In the new physical AI world, of course you have to do sensing, inference, planning, and then actions. All of that AI sits on top of what we have been working on for the last couple of decades. AI never replaces any of the work that we did in the space, it adds on. We have the right portfolio now between the GPUs, NPUs, CPUs, FPGAs, and adaptive SOCs to put that whole system together to do the entire stack and all the workloads associated with physical AI. That's a great segue into our brand new Kria AI robotics developer kit that we've got sitting on the table with us here, which we're really excited to launch here at Advancing AI 2026. Maybe you can just talk a little bit about the Ryzen AI Embedded X100 that's inside of that box and really what we're going to be doing with that new Kria AI robotics developer kit that we're launching here. My pleasure. I mean, look, the X100 is a really phenomenal product. It brings together a whole bunch of our portfolio IP, including our CPUs and GPUs. It's got Zen 5 CPU cores, 16 of them, and RDNA 3.5 cores, 40 of them. We've got NPUs in there all together into a single device under unified memory. If you think about how we've done it, it's a really balanced architecture between the right amount of CPU and GPU to put together for the humanoid brain. And we didn't stop there. We actually produced a SOM module, and that SOM module is with me here. Unfortunately, you can't see it because it's got a heat shield on both sides, but it's got the X100 on top of it. This SOM module can fit into any of the carrier boards that will have sensor fusion on them. And we went further. We actually have the Kria AI box, which is a developer platform, and it's got the entire stack with an FPGA carrier board that does sensor fusion, the Kria X100 SOM, and it is completely open source with the software stack. So people like G-Bionics now can develop and be very, very productive with this platform. That just leads right into my next question, Daniele. Maybe talk a little bit about how you guys are going to use this kind of a platform in the development of humanoids, what you see from AMD from a robotics standpoint, and what that means to you guys from an end-user perspective. Yeah. I mean, these platforms are going to revolutionize how we treat data processing at the edge. Sometimes we don't really realize how humanoid robots need edge processing to compute information from distributed sensing like skin, which can perceive not only touch. Our Gene, for instance, perceives proximity or temperature, which gives a completely different level of interpretation for the robot. So you really need edge computation that can process all this information together with distributed cameras at the edge, so that you can bring outwards rationalized information for the robot to take the best action. These products are going to be fundamental for processing high-bandwidth sensors at the edge so that the robot and physical AI can act optimally with respect to the environment and humans. In this respect, AMD is a fantastic partner for Generative Bionics because we can cover the entire compute stack. We can go from the edge, to the CPU and GPU processing, the inference for reinforcement learning or imitation learning, up to the cloud. This is fundamental because with one single partner you can fine-tune what you need to be effective for the customers, because that's what matters at the end. I want to dig into one other point that you touched on, which is that with physical AI you're actually interacting in the human world with human beings in a real environment. Obviously, that comes with certain risks, and safety is critical when you're interacting with human beings. Can you maybe talk a little bit about how important safety is and what AMD brings to the table for you as a developer as it relates fundamentally to security and how we earn your trust in that type of environment interacting with human beings? Yes, I mean, when you try to make a humanoid robot that interacts with human beings, the level of safety has to be brought to another threshold, another level. And this is not only on the methodology of how the robot interacts, but also how it deals with emergencies. Just to make an example, if the robot at a certain point realizes that it has to shut down, you have to find a way to recover or control the fall. If you have a child in front of you, even if you are falling because you have to shut down, you have to control your fall. That's why a brake is not safety in the case of humanoid robotics. You cannot simply stop, otherwise you may fall. With AMD, we have the chance to revolutionize and bring the safety level to another stage for allowing human-robot integration, because that is what we have to face. Humanoids and humans have to be thought of as a single society, and all safety has to be viewed with that perspective. Salil, can you maybe talk a little bit about how we bake that level of safety into our silicon and our software stacks and everything that we do to ensure that we deliver that level of safety in our products to customers like Daniele? Yeah, I think like Daniele said, this is safety at a whole other level. We have been working in the safety space for a long time at the chip level, but at this point, safety needs to be considered at the system level. The entire architecture and how the whole solution gets put together between the brain, the spine, and the control loops at the edge have to all work synchronously, and safety has to be thought through the entire system. One of the elements of safety is also deterministic control loops that have to finish within a certain fixed amount of time. You need to finish the control loops closer to the data and closer to the endpoints, and you need to make sure that information is transmitted through the spine to the brain, and the entire system works within a fixed period so that you can react faster. Daniele's example about not stopping, but falling in the right way, is a classic example of how that safety loop needs to finish within a deterministic amount of time and be reacted to in the right fashion. So the chips and products that we have have safety built in, but the architecture is now more critical in terms of how we put it all together. One of the other things that we see in physical AI is the fact that there's really not one single company that can deliver absolutely everything you need to make your robot work. From an AMD perspective, obviously, we're working very hard on the silicon, the software stack, and everything like that. But Daniele, if you can just talk about the importance of an ecosystem, a network of partners, and how that's been leveraged for you as you've been developing Gene, and then Salil, maybe you can follow on to talk about what that means from an AMD perspective. Yeah, so physical AI and humanoid robotics is not a sprint, it's a marathon. It's a long journey, and in order to be effective in a long journey, you have to find the right partners that want to build with you. Right now our approach is very simple: we think that one humanoid robot that does it all is not going to work, because one welding humanoid robot is definitely different than a humanoid robot that is in healthcare assisting the elderly. So our approach is very simple, we have to build a platform, and this platform has to generate application-specific humanoid robots for the customers at hand. With AMD, what we're doing is customizing the entire solutions that we have for the customers so that we can meet their KPIs at the production level. Otherwise, humanoid robotics is not going to be sustainable. I would say that at AMD we've taken two approaches to partnering with customers. One is we are very open-source oriented. Everything that we do on the software stack—embedded ROCm, for example, and the robotics stack—we are open-sourcing all of that so people can actually join us, engage with us, and enhance the software stack. Even the hardware that we are building, the SOM that we have, and the developer platform that you see here is fully open source. We are going to give away the schematics of all the boards and everything that you see here so people can customize it to build it for their needs. At the same time, we have a partner program going on across the entire spectrum of things: across the models that people build, the simulation environments, and the software support as well. So we are bringing in the community because, like Daniele has said, it's really a marathon, and we need to be together with our partners and customers to make sure that we fully capitalize on the potential that we have here in physical AI. So my final question for both of you here as we wrap up this really exciting panel: obviously there's a lot of excitement built here today around physical AI, and you can hear all the robots running around out there on the stage. I just want to get both of your perspectives on this inflection point that we're at right now. When people in the industry look back five years from now at this point in time, what do you really think that they'll say was the real turning point in terms of making physical AI a reality? Daniele, I'll start with you. Yeah, I think that when people look back in five years they'll say, okay, remember when humanoid robots were basically still platforms in development, and now we can go to the store and basically buy them and install them at home? That is my view of how the market will expand for humanoid robots, and we are on the right path. I would say that we'll look back and we'll see this as the moment when it all started. As you know, it is going to be a long journey, but we are on the right path, and customers and partners that are more open to working with each other and having an open ecosystem is going to make the huge difference between us having a small pie or all of us actually succeeding together. Great. Well, thank you both. We've covered a lot of ground today talking about the convergence of AI and robotics, how computing platforms make physical AI possible, and all the partnerships that are going to make that a reality. So Salil, Daniele, I really want to thank both of you for sharing your insights, and thank you for being here with us today at Advancing AI 2026. Thank you. Thank you.

Bill Ackman: Harvard's Future, His Iconic X Posts & His Daughter's Recovery | KMP Ep.59

Katie Miller Pod(76.7K)·40:34·2026-10-20
Katie MillerHost, Katie Miller Podcast Bill AckmanInvestor
TL;DRBillionaire investor Bill Ackman discusses his shift from lifelong independent/centrist donor to outspoken political and social commentator on X, his philanthropic efforts in neuro-rehabilitation following his daughter’s near-fatal medical emergency, and his evolving perspectives on higher education, DEI, and the tech industry.
Political Identity: Bill Ackman clarifies he was never a lifelong Democrat; he has always been a centrist who registered with parties in New York primarily to participate in primaries. Critique of the Left: Characterizes the modern Democratic Party as being in the worst...

TL;DR: Billionaire investor Bill Ackman discusses his shift from lifelong independent/centrist donor to outspoken political and social commentator on X, his philanthropic efforts in neuro-rehabilitation following his daughter’s near-fatal medical emergency, and his evolving perspectives on higher education, DEI, and the tech industry.

Political Evolution & The State of the Democratic Party

  • Political Identity: Bill Ackman clarifies he was never a lifelong Democrat; he has always been a centrist who registered with parties in New York primarily to participate in primaries.
  • Critique of the Left:
    • Characterizes the modern Democratic Party as being in the worst place in its 250-year history, describing it as radicalized and "anti-American."
    • Notes that politicians compromise their values to avoid being primaried by the party's progressive wing.
  • Personal Friendships: Maintains personal relationships with figures like Cory Booker despite political disagreements, emphasizing that he does not choose friends based on party affiliation.

Higher Education, DEI, & The Value of a Degree

  • Harvard and Meritocracy:
    • Cites a Harvard professor friend noting that the student body has shifted back toward a meritocracy with less self-censorship, crediting President Alan Garber for fostering a more open-ended environment.
    • Notes that October 7th acted as a "reveal" of underlying ideological rot within elite universities.
  • Corporate DEI: Asserts that corporate America has completely rethought DEI, realizing many practices violated the law. He predicts fewer pride logos during June.
  • Is College Still Worth It?
    • Questions whether his seven-year-old daughter will attend a conventional university, pointing out that a Harvard education now costs roughly $400,000 for four years.
    • Criticizes the lack of financial disclosure for 18-year-olds taking on massive student debt, particularly noting that student loans are non-dischargeable in bankruptcy.
    • Highlights institutions like Vanderbilt—led by Daniel Diermeyer—as rising alternatives prioritizing Western values, free speech, and academic excellence.

Impact on X (Twitter) & Public Discourse

  • The "Long Bangers": Known for publishing extensive, multi-thousand-word posts (such as a 4,600-word post on DEI in January 2024), Ackman states his goal is simply "speaking the truth."
  • Handling Criticism: Claims he is unafraid of negative feedback or job loss because he is financially independent, noting that he cares deeply about his reputation only when negative claims are true.

Family Life, Health Battles, & Philanthropy

  • Marriage to Neri Oxman: Met Neri Oxman after a former Harvard professor introduced them, following a three-month courtship entirely via phone before their first date.
  • Daughter Lucy’s Medical Crisis:
    • Discusses his daughter Lucy’s recovery from a near-fatal brain hemorrhage caused by an AVM (arteriovenous malformation) after being on the ground for 15–16 hours.
    • Notes she has made remarkable cognitive progress despite severe visual impairment and ongoing rehabilitation for speech and mobility.
  • Neuro-Rehab Philanthropy:
    • Recruited a team from Mount Sinai to build a philanthropic neuro-rehab and research ecosystem.
    • Aims to bypass the broken economic reimbursement models of traditional hospital neurosurgery to focus heavily on long-term patient recovery and basic science.

Rapid-Fire Insights & Miscellaneous

  • China and BCI: Expresses concern over China's major advances in biotechnology and Brain-Computer Interfaces (BCI), noting their streamlined drug approval pipelines.
  • Business Ventures: Randomly bought British watch company Bremont after walking into a store, writing a note to the owners, and eventually acquiring the company.
  • Dream Dinner Party: Founding fathers George Washington, Thomas Jefferson, and Benjamin Franklin, eating wild boar.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Bill Ackman: Predicts that there will be fewer pride flags displayed on corporate logos during the month of June going forward.
  • Bill Ackman: Predicts that brain-computer interfaces (BCI) and wearable technology will eventually allow his daughter Lucy and others with optic nerve damage to see and speak again.

CME's Terry Duffy on the Disconcerting State of Finance Regulation | Odd Lots

Bloomberg Podcasts(597.0K)·28:29·7.8K views·2026-10-20
Terry DuffyCEO, CME Group
TL;DRTerry Duffy reflects on his 46-year career at the CME Group, warning against the degradation of financial regulation, the risks of unregulated perpetual futures and prediction markets, and the inevitability of 24/7 trading pending banking infrastructure updates.
Historical Context: Terry Duffy began his career when the CFTC was a brand-new, principles-based agricultural oversight agency; over five decades, it expanded to cover financial futures, equity indexes, and crypto. Current Regulatory Concerns: Duffy characterizes the current...

TL;DR

Terry Duffy reflects on his 46-year career at the CME Group, warning against the degradation of financial regulation, the risks of unregulated perpetual futures and prediction markets, and the inevitability of 24/7 trading pending banking infrastructure updates.

Regulatory Evolution and the State of Oversight

  • Historical Context: Terry Duffy began his career when the CFTC was a brand-new, principles-based agricultural oversight agency; over five decades, it expanded to cover financial futures, equity indexes, and crypto.
  • Current Regulatory Concerns: Duffy characterizes the current regulatory environment as a "ready, fire-aim strategy," noting that under the second Trump administration, 2,500 self-certifications have been submitted to the CFTC without a single denial.
  • Vulnerability of Agency Rules: Duffy warns that rules and regulations enacted by agencies like the CFTC and SEC are not sustainable because they can be easily wiped away by the stroke of a pen when a new administration takes office.

Prediction Markets and Market Credibility

  • Criticism of Trivial Contracts: Duffy criticizes prediction markets that violate core principle three of the Commodity Exchange Act (susceptibility to manipulation), citing absurd approved contracts such as Nathan's hot dog eating contests, George Santos's attendance at the State of the Union, and Donald Trump's teleprompter.
  • Opposition to Insider Information: Arguing against the idea that insider information on prediction platforms has informational value, Duffy states that level-playing-field information is fundamental to fair markets, comparing privileged prediction feeds to paid access on Truth Social.
  • Legitimate Alternatives: Duffy contrasts unregulated prediction bets with FutureSports—an index allowing teams to monetize brand value (such as a sponsorship deal with the Chicago White Sox and recent deals with the NBA and MLB)—arguing this provides an economic ecosystem rather than mere consumer gambling.

Perpetual Futures, Leverage, and Offshore Platforms

  • Legal and Tax Status of Perps: Duffy explains why CME litigation targets perpetual futures: perps function as swaps (two parties paying each other via a funding rate) rather than futures, yet attempt to claim favorable 1256 tax treatment (60% long-term, 40% short-term) without complying with swap dealer and clearing regulations.
  • Hyperliquid and Offshore Risks: Discussing offshore blockchain-based perps platforms like Hyperliquid, Duffy highlights systemic risks including zero KYC ("know your customer"), auto-liquidation, and extreme leverage reaching up to 250%, which can trigger cascading market-moving events.
  • Real Motive for Perps: Duffy argues that perpetuals are being pushed into the U.S. strictly for leverage and retail speculation rather than concerns over contract roll dates.

Asset Classes and 24/7 Market Inevitability

  • GPU Futures Potential: Duffy defends GPU unit futures (developed with partner Don Wilson), noting that while skeptics question the asset class, the GPU market will be multiples the size of crypto by 2030, exceeding a projected trillion-dollar valuation.
  • Banking Infrastructure Bottleneck: Duffy asserts that 24/7 trading is ultimately inevitable, but warns that the broader $76 trillion U.S. equity market cannot trade continuously until the underlying banking system supports 24/7 pre-funding and real-time payments.

Career Reflections and Crisis Moments

  • Discipline and Exit Strategies: Reflecting on going broke with a $150,000 borrowed loss in 1981, Duffy advises young traders to maintain a moral compass, accept when they are wrong, and always establish a clear exit strategy before entering a trade.
  • Scariest Career Moment: Duffy identifies the April 20, 2020 pandemic crash—where oil prices plunged to negative $37.50—as the scariest moment of his career due to the sudden evaporation of demand and the feeling of someone yelling "fire" in a crowded theater.

Episode 57: David Gross - Managing Partner of Bain Capital

Generating Alpha Podcast(3.3K)·51:22·82 views·2026-10-20
David GrossManaging Partner, Bain Capital
TL;DRDavid Gross reflects on his 25-year career scaling Bain Capital in Asia—including executing the $18 billion Kioxia turnaround—and shares insights from his transition in January to serving as the firm's first sole managing partner since Mitt Romney.
Upbringing: David Gross grew up in suburban and rural parts of Ohio and upstate New York as a twin in a family of four children. Wharton & Japan Inspiration: Attended Wharton in the late 1980s during the height of the "Japan is number one" economic era; took a mentor's...

TL;DR

David Gross reflects on his 25-year career scaling Bain Capital in Asia—including executing the $18 billion Kioxia turnaround—and shares insights from his transition in January to serving as the firm's first sole managing partner since Mitt Romney.

Background & Early Career in Japan

  • Upbringing: David Gross grew up in suburban and rural parts of Ohio and upstate New York as a twin in a family of four children.
  • Wharton & Japan Inspiration: Attended Wharton in the late 1980s during the height of the "Japan is number one" economic era; took a mentor's suggestion to visit Japan through a rural homestay program and a Mitsubishi research stint.
  • Corporate Japan Realities (1992): Joined NEC Corporation in Tokyo just as the economic bubble burst, working on the 40th floor of a 60-floor headquarters.
      • Observations: Witnessed the dichotomy between a highly flat, bottom-up manufacturing floor utilizing *kaizen* and continuous improvement, and a rigid, slow-moving white-collar management structure that struggled with software, the internet, and knowledge-worker efficiency.

Building Bain Capital Asia

  • Entering the Asian Market (2000–2006): Joined Bain Capital in 2000; opened the Tokyo office in 2006 when most Western investors had abandoned Japan.
      • Strategic Pivot: Shifted away from complex, globalized tech giants facing macro headwinds (like NEC and Toyota) to domestic service, consumer, and food businesses with high defensive moats and turnaround potential.
  • Regional Differentiation: Adapted the U.S. buyout model across vastly different Asian ecosystems:
      • China: Initially a minority growth-oriented market.
      • Japan: Focused on control-oriented buyouts.
      • India: Partnered with businesses seeking global scale and operational best practices.
  • Fund Evolution: Raised the first Asia fund at $1 billion in 2007, later expanding the platform into credit, special situations, and life sciences.

The Kioxia (Toshiba Memory) Saga

  • Navigating High-Stakes Complexity: Executed the defining deal of his career by beating out KKR, Western Digital, and Foxconn for Toshiba's crown-jewel memory business.
  • Key Hurdles Overcome: Kept the Japanese government and Ministry of Economy, Trade and Industry (METI) comfortable, survived seven months of Chinese antitrust review during a U.S.–China trade war, and resolved ongoing litigation with joint-venture partner Western Digital by bringing in Apple, Dell, and Seagate as part of a consortium.
  • Financial Outcome: Survived years of cyclical downturns where the business generated zero EBITDA before capitalizing on the AI and hyperscaler boom; Bain fully exited the deal this summer with over $15 billion in returns and $45 billion in net income/EBITDA generated.
  • Cultural Payoff: Maintained trust with Japanese banks by prioritizing debt recovery on earlier, tougher deals, which unlocked flexible financing and support during Kioxia's darkest periods.

Leadership at Bain Capital & Firm Governance

  • Taking the Helm: Became the sole managing partner of Bain Capital in January, the first person to run the firm alone since Mitt Romney after spending 25 years working alongside the founding generation.
  • Private vs. Public Status: Emphasized that Bain Capital will remain private to protect its unique partnership culture, maintain alignment with long-term principal investors, and avoid public market pressures for short-term fee revenue.
  • AI Integration: Implementing an internal AI model trained on Bain's historical deal data to overcome investor blind spots, challenge pattern recognition biases, and improve valuation frameworks.

Notable Quotes

*"Most of these decisions and these events are not the result of a linear process of systematic thought, but there's a bit of an opportunity and a practical bet that you're making that could work out."* — David Gross
*"This area of the world can be kind of misleading and people thinking that it is very driven by the numbers and by capital and finance and such, and at the end of the day it's not really. It's a very much people-driven and a relationship-driven business."* — David Gross
This week on generating alpha, I sat down with David Gross, managing partner of Bang Capital, one of the world's leading private investment firms with approximately 215 billion in assets under management. David grew up between Ohio and upstate New York. At Wharton, he became fascinated with Japan and on a mentor suggestion lended in Tokyo in 1992 to join NEC just as the bubble was bursting. After HBS and Bane and Company, he joined Bane Capital in 2000 and opened its Tokyo offices in 2006 when most Western investors had given up on Japan. He stayed 25 years, did 45 deals without a single bankruptcy and built Asia into one multi-asset platform. His defining deal was Kioa. He beat KKR, Western Digital, and Foxcon. Survived seven months of Chinese antitrust review during a trade war and held on as the memory market crashed and the deal set underwater for years. This summer, Bane fully exited with over 15 billion dollars in returns from that deal. In January, David became the first person to run Bane Capital alone since Mitt Romney. In our conversation, we talked about arriving in Japan as the party was ending, why he stayed when everyone else left, buying the company that gave him his first job, the Kiosa Saga, lessons from Bane's founding generation, why performance matters more than AUM, and his advice for a 17-year-old today. Google CEO Sundar Pichai: AI’s Infinite Possibilities, Compute Scarcity Dilemmas, Future AI Products

Alex Kantrowitz(70.0K)·45:30·2026-10-20
Sundar PichaiCEO, Google and Alphabet Alex KantrowitzHost, Big Technology Podcast
TL;DRGoogle CEO Sundar Pichai discussed the launch of the frontier Gemini 4 Argon model, the company's compute allocation strategy, and the future of agentic AI interfaces.
Pichai addressed industry-wide compute scarcity, recursive self-improvement, and Google's multi-trillion token adoption rates since Argon's release. Frontier Commitment: Sundar Pichai emphasized that staying at the AI frontier is in Google's DNA and provides an early look into...

TL;DR

Google CEO Sundar Pichai discussed the launch of the frontier Gemini 4 Argon model, the company's compute allocation strategy, and the future of agentic AI interfaces. Pichai addressed industry-wide compute scarcity, recursive self-improvement, and Google's multi-trillion token adoption rates since Argon's release.

Gemini 4 Argon Launch and Model Performance

  • Frontier Commitment: Sundar Pichai emphasized that staying at the AI frontier is in Google's DNA and provides an early look into emerging capabilities, helping the company prepare for future agentic and long-horizon tasks.
  • Rapid Adoption Metrics: Sundar Pichai reported that in the first 72 hours following its launch, Gemini 4 Argon reached nearly 25 trillion tokens used, approximately 3x the volume of Gemini 3.0 during its initial 72 hours.
  • Coding Efficiency Improvements: Sundar Pichai noted that four weeks post-launch, Argon has driven a 30% increase in coding output and a one-third decrease in rollbacks for tasks affecting millions of lines of code.

Compute Allocation and Scarcity

  • Multi-Year Planning Constraints: Sundar Pichai explained that current compute availability is a function of decisions made in 2024, and acknowledged that the industry is broadly short on compute with demand extending into 2027.
  • Cloud Revenue Trade-offs: Sundar Pichai stated that Google's cloud business routinely foregoes short-term revenue to prioritize compute allocation for frontier AI research and development.
  • Infrastructure Bottlenecks: Sundar Pichai noted that building compute involves over 2,000 components, creating potential macroeconomic risks and supply chain constraints spanning memory, electricity, and labor.

Safety, Alignment, and Recursive Self-Improvement (RSI)

  • Current State of RSI: Sundar Pichai clarified that current recursive self-improvement relies on AI agents assisting human researchers as collaborators within development loops rather than fully autonomous intelligence explosions.
  • Defense-in-Depth Architecture: Sundar Pichai outlined Google's safety framework, which combines alignment research, real-time monitoring telemetry, and sandboxed, isolated architectures to supervise autonomous model execution.
  • Industry Standards Call: Sundar Pichai advocated for the creation of cross-industry standards and independent evaluators akin to financial accounting and auditing to safely govern deployed models.

AI Interfaces, Products, and Ecosystem Partnerships

  • Unified Agent Architecture: Sundar Pichai described upcoming consumer rollouts of persistent, 24/7 cloud agents that spin up microVMs per query to unify experiences across Search, Workspace, Gmail, and the Gemini app.
  • Anthropic and Apple Partnerships: Sundar Pichai highlighted Google's early assessment of Anthropic's world-class founding team and confirmed that Google's partnership with Apple to integrate customized Gemini models into Siri is performing exceptionally well.
  • Space-Based Data Centers: Sundar Pichai revealed that Google is 2 to 3 years into exploring sun-synchronous orbit data centers to harness 24/7 solar energy, noting that the team recently sent TPUs to space to await a "hello world" milestone.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Sundar Pichai predicts that computing paradigms will shift away from humans adapting to screens toward technology seamlessly adapting to human daily life through persistent, multi-agent frameworks.
  • Sundar Pichai expects progress on recursive self-improvement loops to continue advancing steadily into next year.

How AI Is Repricing Every Software Company

Invest Like The Best(113.0K)·64:57·26.0K views·2026-10-20
Christina CacioppoCo-founder and CEO, Vanta Patrick O'ShaughnessyHost, Invest Like The Best
TL;DRVanta co-founder and CEO Christina Cacioppo discusses surviving the "SaaSpocalypse," reinventing software around AI agents, navigating soaring compensation in talent wars, and building an enduring cybersecurity and trust platform in an era of low tech-trust.
Founding Premise: Started 8 years ago by Christina Cacioppo to help companies build, mature, and earn security compliance (SOC 2, ISO 27001, HIPAA) and trust centers. Current Scale: Reached 16,000 global customers with five distinct security products. Data Foundation...

TL;DR

Vanta co-founder and CEO Christina Cacioppo discusses surviving the "SaaSpocalypse," reinventing software around AI agents, navigating soaring compensation in talent wars, and building an enduring cybersecurity and trust platform in an era of low tech-trust.

Vanta's Evolution and the "Trust Graph" Foundation

  • Founding Premise: Started 8 years ago by Christina Cacioppo to help companies build, mature, and earn security compliance (SOC 2, ISO 27001, HIPAA) and trust centers.
  • Current Scale: Reached 16,000 global customers with five distinct security products.
  • Data Foundation Advantage:
      • Connected Vanta to over 400 software integrations.
      • Built the "Trust Graph"—a security data lake that pulls massive operational data to power downstream automation, agents, and AI.

Surviving the "SaaSpocalypse" & The AI Pricing Trap

  • Cognitive Dissonance in Valuations: Vanta maintains high growth and ~80% margins (ideals of the previous era), yet valuation multiples have compressed significantly as capital concentrates on AI infrastructure labs and autonomous solutions.
  • The Token Pricing Trap:
      • Token Markups: Christina Cacioppo warns against marking up raw model tokens (e.g., 4x on a Claude token) because customers quickly compare prices against the underlying models and reject the value proposition.
      • Credit Confusion: Obscuring token costs behind complex credit-token exchange rates also fails because users are too sophisticated.
      • Current Strategy: Vanta currently "eats the token cost" and takes lower margins to prioritize agent usage, deployment, and long-term retention.

AI Agents, Codebases, and Enterprise Readiness

  • Agentic Product Experience: Vanta's platform is shifting toward long-running agents that actively perform work, generate UI on the fly, and prompt users for permission (e.g., automated offboarding, access removal). Currently, 25% of customers are in this agentic flow; the goal is 75%.
  • Codebase Modularization: Maintaining an 8-year-old codebase required modularizing smaller components so coding agents have sufficiently small context windows to understand and modify code effectively without high-context engineer babysitting.
  • Overcoming LLM Fear in Product Teams: Product teams initially defaulted to building old-school onboarding wizards out of fear of LLM failure. Management forced a pivot to an LLM-driven canvas where users drag messy files in to auto-configure compliance.

Talent Wars and Exploding Compensation

  • Displacement of Comp Bands: Traditional compensation bands are "a joke" relative to what top talent can command.
  • 10x Comp Multiplier: Total compensation for top-tier, high-agency employees at Vanta has increased by a factor of 10 over the past five years.
  • Philosophy on Talent: Christina Cacioppo argues that top people are never paid enough; high-agency individuals who make reasonable decisions, iterate quickly on mistakes, and own outcomes are extraordinarily valuable.

Security at Machine Speed

  • Persistent AI Threats: Autonomous models attempt millions of variations to chain together benign or low-severity vulnerabilities into critical exploits.
  • Automated Remediation: The future of security requires continuous machine-speed penetration testing paired with automated patcher agents that fix issues instantly, bypassing traditional 90-day prioritization queues.

Notable Quotes

*"You're really only as good as the last thing you shipped. And so you can be first and you can start it and doesn't really matter... someone can vibe code your thing in a weekend."* — Christina Cacioppo
*"Anyone who is marking up Claude tokens 4x and trying to pass that off to their users... that is not a model. We're eating the token cost right now."* — Christina Cacioppo
*"My general comp philosophy is like you never pay the top people enough and you should keep... a bunch of people you pay much more than their contribution."* — Christina Cacioppo

How Toto Wolff built the most valuable team in F1

Cheeky Pint(17.6K)·71:54·2026-10-20
Toto WolffTeam Principal and Co-Owner, Mercedes-AMG PETRONAS F1 Team John CollisonHost, Cheeky Pint
TL;DRToto Wolff breaks down how Mercedes spots generational F1 drivers, enforces a strict "No Dickheads" culture across 2,500 employees, and navigates F1's strict cost cap to build the most valuable team in the sport.
Early Karting Indicators: Toto Wolff explains that racing talent is identifiable very early—sometimes seeing an 8-year-old beat 10-year-olds in karting, or watching a child comfortably slide a kart at 100 kilometers per hour in the rain. The Unquantifiable Element: Unlike...

TL;DR

Toto Wolff breaks down how Mercedes spots generational F1 drivers, enforces a strict "No Dickheads" culture across 2,500 employees, and navigates F1's strict cost cap to build the most valuable team in the sport.

Talent Spotting & Driver Evaluation

  • Early Karting Indicators: Toto Wolff explains that racing talent is identifiable very early—sometimes seeing an 8-year-old beat 10-year-olds in karting, or watching a child comfortably slide a kart at 100 kilometers per hour in the rain.
  • The Unquantifiable Element: Unlike cycling metrics (such as Tadej Pogačar's watt-per-kilogram threshold or VO2 max), driving success relies heavily on unmeasurable personality traits like resilience and teamwork.
  • The "Speed" and Connectedness: Speed is defined by billions of neurosensors allowing a driver to "strap the car onto themselves." Toto Wolff notes that Lewis Hamilton famously described a car understeering "from the front axle to where I sit."

Organizational Culture & Talent Management

  • The "No Dickheads" Policy: Borrowed from the All Blacks over 10 years ago, Toto Wolff enforces a strict cultural filter across Mercedes' 2,500 employees (split into 150 trackside staff and 2,350 engineering/operational personnel across two UK hubs for engines and chassis), rejecting brilliant engineers who lack humility, integrity, and honesty.
  • Team Leadership & Scale: Toto Wolff contrasts his founder-led approach with other F1 teams like Ferrari (where the team principal is one of 10 direct reports to the CEO) and Red Bull, noting his long leash and lack of fear of being fired.

Economics, Cap Ex, and Valuations

  • The Cost Cap Era: Toto Wolff notes that the $200M+ annual cost cap introduced by F1 eliminated long-term speculative R&D and lab work, forcing teams to weigh compute power and upgrades against current and next-season lap-time gains.
  • Financial Performance: Toto Wolff reveals that Mercedes F1 generates approximately $1 billion in revenue with a 30% profit margin ($300 million EBIT).
  • Valuation Surge: Mercedes F1 was valued at £4.6 billion in recent transactions (reflecting a roughly 15x EBIT multiple), representing a 7x increase from previous years, driven by scarcity and a growing demographic.
  • Demographic Shifts: Driven heavily by *Drive to Survive*, F1's audience now boasts 42% female fans, with the 15-25 age bracket experiencing the fastest growth.

Regulations, Loophole Engineering, and Technical Risk

  • The Mock Trial Process: When Mercedes develops a grey-area innovation, leading engineers and Toto Wolff hold a mock trial where inventors act as prosecutors, and a general counsel defends the part before deciding whether to deploy it or consult the FIA.
  • Historic Innovations: Toto Wolff details the "DAS" (Dual-Axis Steering) system—where drivers could push and pull the steering wheel to adjust front-wheel toe effects and heat tires—which was permitted for one year before being banned.

Early Venture Investing & Dot-Com Exits

  • Dot-Com Portfolio: Before racing, Toto Wolff ran a private holding company investing in tech startups, including a free SMS web portal (`sms.at`) started by a teenager in Graz that scaled to 2 million users in a 9-million-person Austria.
  • The 2000 Exit: After selling `sms.at` to T-Mobile for €250 million, a conversation with his CFO on a flight from Bonn to Vienna prompted Toto Wolff to liquidate 8 of his 9 tech investments just as the dot-com bubble burst in spring 2000.

Notable Quotes

"The stopwatch never lies. But put them in the same car, the stopwatch is going to decide whether you're being judged good or not. But underneath the stopwatch, there needs to be technical ability... and what we call speed." — Toto Wolff
"We've learned from the All Blacks... and they have a saying, which is on their training ground, that says, 'No dickheads.' And we have continued to run that motto." — Toto Wolff

Many people across many jobs are in the talent spotting business, but you're especially in the talent spotting business. How do you actually spot a Kimi? How do I spot a Kimi? I think when it comes to spotting racing drivers, we can judge pretty early on in a kid's karting performance whether that kid has the potential to be a professional racing driver. Even with the tiny ones, you can identify the ones that are doing better than their peers, either because they are younger. If someone that's 8 years old beats the ones that are 10 in that group, you can have those very early signs of whether that kid has the potential. You can see when someone is comfortable in the kart, going at 100 kilometers an hour in the rain, and that kid does his first lap and comes across the corner sideways... "Okay, there are the ingredients for being good at it." How quantitative is it? In certain sports... In cycling, there's a metric called threshold power and Tadej Pogačar, the best in the world, is very clearly just the best in the world on this single metric of how much power, for your weight, can you produce. Or is there some number that Kimi and George Russell would stand out on compared to other racers? This is what makes our sport so unique. There isn't any. With Tadej Pogačar, you can see that that threshold, watt per kilogram or VO2 max, these are things that are measurable. But, I don't know what Tadej's metrics were when he was 12. Maybe that was not best-in-class. Maybe that came later. The ingredients of success that you can't measure personality. How resilient are you to pressure? How much can you play the team game? When it comes to racing drivers, there are obviously similar KPIs, the stopwatch. The stopwatch never lies. But put them in the same car, the stopwatch is going to decide whether you're being judged good or not. But underneath the stopwatch, there needs to be technical ability to set your car up in the right way, interact with the engineers, have some kind of social competence of making sure you have sponsors, treat them well. There have been extremely talented kids that never went anywhere because they were just simply bad people or not intelligent enough. Then there is the overwhelming part, and that is what we call speed. What is speed? All of your cognitive abilities and neurosensors, the sheer billions of sensors that a human has, that give you an ability that not many have. It's actually to feel what the car does underneath of you. You can see with the extraordinary drivers, they feel much more. They feel more connected to the car on the road than anybody else. One example is an old friend and driver of mine, Lewis Hamilton. You have two axles—a front axle, a rear axle—and you have the chassis in between. When you call a car understeer, it means when you turn the wheel and it goes straight. That happens over the tires and the front axle. It's pretty normal. But Lewis once said something when talking about the car: "The car understeers from the front axle to where I sit." That's the middle of the car. How can that understeer? The engineers couldn't really identify what he meant, but that is what his body told him or his brain told him the car is doing. If a driver is able to have that underlying layer of ability, then that becomes unbeatable. But that is genetics. The great drivers strap the car onto themselves. They don't sit in the car and there's a connectedness. Absolutely. Connectedness is the right word. It's actually being one with the car and with the road. How did you decide that you weren't good enough to be a professional driver, on this topic of talent identification? That's one decision. Talking about humiliation and trauma as ingredients for later success. When I was a child or a teenager, there wasn't really anything differentiating me or outstanding, be it academically or in sports or socially. When I was 18, by sheer coincidence, I ended up with a friend on a racetrack watching an F3 race, which is a junior formula. When I stepped into this paddock, it's like I've gotten my identity. This is what I wanted to be. I wanted to be a racing driver, someone that is able to tame this machine. This is when I started... Basically my career as a driver, but I came too late to the party. I didn't have all of this karting background that teaches you the fundamentals of the physics of how to position your car in the race. But I became a pro. I went through the various stages of junior Formula Racing into what we call GT cars, touring cars or sports cars with a certain amount of success. It is like you do about your business, I guess. What I'm doing today is I... Woke up with that desire and I went to bed with that desire. That lasted for quite a few years. The other, maybe less talked about part, obviously, there's a huge amount of fixation on driver selection and who will get the next seat and everything like that. Presumably, a lot of what you spend time on is recruiting the right engineers and aerodynamicists and things like that. How does spotting that kind of talent work? Are you trying to pull people from other Formula One teams or from other industries and outside? Just what have you learned about talent outside of drivers? When you look at the Formula One team from outside as a fan, you see two drivers. You see then a guy like me who is sitting in front of a screen, and then you see the mechanics, and sometimes the engineers on the pit wall. This is what you expect to be the racing team. But this is just the tip of the iceberg. It's about 150 people that go to the tracks, including marketing. It's 90 technical, operational people. But we are two-and-a-half thousand. Here in the UK, we have two hubs, one that does the engine, the other one, what we call the chassis. That is the works team. We are 2,500 people in an organization that is quite vast if you look at the two cars. For me, the selection process of the people that I want to work with is the same or is similar in terms of the pattern for racing drivers. I always say there's not two superstars in the team. There's two-and-a-half thousand, and I treat all of them equally. We've learned from the All Blacks, 10 or more years earlier, and they have a saying, which is on their training ground, that says, "No dickheads." And we have continued to run that motto. The extraordinary scientist or engineer that hasn't got the right values of humility, integrity, honesty, enthusiasm will have no future in the organization. We're trying to filter that out pretty early. Sometimes you find out later, but no place for the genius asshole. You're describing a lot the need for people to collaborate within the organization and the teamwork. But what's interesting is you have these different domains where you have the aerodynamics, and you have the battery systems, and you have the internal combustion powertrain and traction and everything. The R&D almost entirely goes towards cars where you know the rules. There isn't much room for general R&D because you just don't know. You're not going to work on batteries before you know what exactly will be permitted when it comes to batteries and things like that. You have to know the target that you're designing against. We did it a long time ago, but Formula One introduced the cost cap a few years ago. Every resource that you deploy into engineering is being counted for in the cost cap, which is $200 million and a bit a year. Therefore, long-term R&D work, lab work, is actually, unfortunately, very limited because you need to decide between deploying compute power in a front wing that you might have in three months against some long-term feature. For all F1 teams, one of the things the cost cap changed is speculative R&D just had to go because ultimately you have fixed dollars, and you have to put absolutely everything you can towards this season and next season. I wouldn't say all of it had to go. You still need to find the right balance between what you think future cars are going to benefit from, value the lap-time effect it can have versus the current cars. Unfortunately, that percentage of R&D development, of science and lab work, which I love, has a more limited scope. You have not only the overall cost cap, but you have things like limits on the number of wind tunnel runs and CFD simulations. Isn't that a bit weird that you can't spend your R&D budget exactly as you want to? There are two sides of that coin. The first one is to say, Formula One was always unlimited budget and therefore ultra-sophisticated machinery, which led to a situation that the big teams—Mercedes, Ferrari, Red Bull—were leapfrogging all of the other teams. Because for us, we had those big motherships behind us that benefited from either a shareholder that was extremely keen, like Red Bull, or Mercedes that said, "This is a huge branding exercise for us, and the spending is relatively low in comparison to all of our marketing." What happened is only these three teams actually won races. So Formula One said, "We want everybody to compete on the same budgets." That was the reason why it was introduced. Now, you can see that limited very much the science aspect of Formula One, but it brought also more competition. That's one of the positive effects. The other one is that Formula One, back in the day, Chase Carey, who was the CEO, said, "We need to protect you from yourselves." Because obviously, I was against the cost cap, because I knew that Mercedes, we had might, and that probably the worst case scenario was to lose against a Ferrari or against a Red Bull, but not against a McLaren or Williams or anybody else. He said that to me. Eventually, it was take it or leave it. You are investing, trying to eke out tiny advantages in the car. One of the things that's interesting is that there are the rules, there are things that are clearly against the rules, there are things that are permitted, and then there's a spectrum between those of the gray areas. One of the challenges is you defend something that you think is great and clearly allowed, but then it's banned mid-season, or it's banned in the next season. Was it really worth all that effort to get a trivial advantage? Maybe, maybe not. I think the lesson we've learned is that it needs the dialogue with the governing body. If you feel you have an innovation that passed the first red face test within the organization, that means the leading engineers, sometimes myself on the table, we are having a mock trial. The people that have invented the loophole or the opportunity play the prosecutor, so they need to fight their own argument. On the other side, there are the engineers that defend the innovation, and then there's our general counsel, Carrie, and myself who are the judges. You try to see how convincing the defense is? Yeah, exactly. How convincing is the defense, and how can you destroy the argument that is actually legal? That would be an extreme case where there's something that we struggle with in the engineering community to define. But obviously, the dialogue with the FIA is important and bring them on very early. If you have a very secret proprietary improvement that you don't want to unveil, it will be totally top secret within Mercedes, but you will also bring the FIA into it? If we think it's 100% defendable, crystal-clear defendable, then we will deploy it on the car, and then we will make the argument why it's right. Normally, the FIA will say, "We don't like it." They could say, "We like it, but it circumvents the principle of the regulation, so we're going to ban it because the principle of regulation is the most equal competition." If they're fair, they're going to say, "Ban it next year." If they believe it's really very powerful, and it's not what they wanted to see or intended to define, then it could be banned much earlier. This is where the politics come into play. Basically, if they think it's a fair loophole, they'll give you one season of advantage. I would say this is, call it the experience that we have because they are innovators and the engineers, too, and they will respect if something comes up like this. One example was the system that we call DAS, which was, you have the various dimensions of the steering wheel, what you do. I don't know if you heard about it. You can steer to the left, and you can steer to the right. That part I know. I'm with you so far. That's like in an airplane. No, in an airplane, you can also go forward and back. Exactly. It's actually nothing like an airplane. Here, it becomes interesting. What we did, we created another dimension with the steering wheel, actually to push and to pull the steering wheel in a Formula One car. What it did, it changed— Yes, I do remember this. It changed the way the front wheels were operating in terms of its toe effect. What it did is we were able to turn... We created a toe effect which helped us to heat up the surfaces of the tires by basically dragging them along— You were running them inefficiently. They respected the innovation. I remember the first test, Sky had an onboard and said, "What's happening here? They're pushing and pulling the steering wheel." When it was shown to me four or five months before we actually produced it, they called me to a rig in test and development, and there was the steering wheel, and they said, "Now, turn to the left and turn to the right." I was a driver. Then they said, "Now, push it and pull it." I said, "That doesn't go." It's like I'm used to it in order to fix the steering wheel to where I wanted to have it. But it feels deeply uncomfortable actually to have this additional motion in the steering wheel. The Formula One drivers got in and said, "No, we'd like to try it." And it really worked. Come back, I think if you want to be… You want to have the exchange with the FIA to make sure that you're not on the wrong side of the argument. With that, did they end up banning it, or they ended up permitting it on the team? They banned it after a year. Why did they ban it after a year? Why didn't they say, "Okay, this is one of the things that F1 cars can do now?" I don't know. Maybe we lost the political argument that it was good. Other teams said it would cost so much to replicate it, and they could. But, is that really the intention of the regulations? Why did you do it as a push-pull rather than a button on the steering wheel? Or why was the push-pull the control mechanism? I think there was a regulation that allowed to have a mechanical system that was… The rule is the driver must drive the car. Yes. So it needed to be deployed for the driver through a mechanical system. I believe that was the reason. I see. That was the best way to have it defensible. Yeah. That's so interesting. Where would you categorize the compression ratio kerfuffle, ambient temperature versus measuring a temperature? Where does that fit on this spectrum? This was just other teams feeling sore about not having read the regulations in the right way. Is it hard to keep these top-secret improvements a secret? Yeah, it is because there is a natural fluctuation of personnel between the teams. Would you compartmentalize? It's like the Manhattan Project. You need to firewall things. We firewall certain things, and I'm maybe more on the extreme side within the organization. Not everyone needs to know everything, but eventually, you will always have a group of what do you call it, 20, 25 people— Who need to be in the know. Who need to be in there, and they may change teams, and then that obviously leaks. Can you prevent people from changing teams within a season? There is the regulatory environment of every country. How long can you actually prohibit someone to work for another team? With our contractual frameworks and in the UK, you can do it up to 12 months. But there are countries like Switzerland, for example, where it's three months. You got to be careful who you hire in Switzerland. We haven't got our Audis in Switzerland, for example. You got to be careful who you hire elsewhere. But you always need to account for the fact that people may change teams because of personal reasons. Absolutely fair argument. That's why, how can you make sure that things are not leaking too easily? But they will eventually always come out. If you could write the rules, how would you change them? Well, I'm in two minds. The innovation part in me and listening to my engineers is free rein. We have a budget cap that is 200 million, and then make it as open as you want, because that would be magnificent from a scientific standpoint, an engineering standpoint. But what would happen is the best engineers with the most capability also in terms of infrastructure will come out with a car that is much superior than some of the other teams. It would create a field where the leading car could be five seconds quicker than the next one. That is not what Formula One wants. It's becoming more and more restrictive. Is that good or bad? I think it needs to be sensible because this is still the fastest laboratory in the world. That is in our DNA, and I think that is still happening. Obviously, engineers will always say, "Well, it's not as much fun as it was in the past," but it's still a lot of fun. You were describing one set of risks in developing new technical changes, which is the regulatory risk. Will you be allowed to have it even for one season or certainly multiple? But the other risk is just technical risks. Will it work? I think in any R&D organization... We have this at Stripe where we only have so much time, we only have so much money. We have only so much engineers, and we need to put them... Towards the projects that are most likely to work. You don't know upfront what's going to work. So part of the special sauce is that ability to predict this product direction is a good one versus this one is just… It's not awful, it's just not the best. You're fundamentally playing this game of where you spread the poker chips. How do you manage technical risk? How do you think about allocating those very precious chips? Again, they're even more precious now in the new cost cap era. So, the unfortunate part of it is that your business is far more complex, in my opinion. Why? Because you deal with the end customer or whoever the customer is, an organization, an entity, or a person. You can define technical specifications that you believe make the process better or more efficient or successful. But there is someone at the end that actually needs to buy your product, and that person or that organization is going to measure whether the humans that are actually his customers or her customers are going to like it. How can you predict that part? With us, it's all data and the simulation. It's much more measurable. It's much more measurable. At a very early stage, we will define what is the lap time contribution that we imagine of that part. That all starts in the virtual world. It's all simulation, whether it is CFD, whether it's the wind tunnel in the way we measure it, or we attribute downforce and drag, or whether it is mathematical models that we have. All along the process, you continue to correlate with your expectations. And, there are rare occasions where you believe that something eventually, when it ends up in the car, it's going to be a 10th of a second in average, over a lap, quicker, and you realize it's not, or you realize that the driver doesn't feel comfortable with it and isn't feeling safe enough to drive it. We need to consider also if the end customer, which we know exactly, you maybe not, will be able to extract from that virtual, let's say, theoretical lap-time benefit. But it's much more measurable. Engineers like to fall in love with their ideas and, "I just need one more simulation run," or, "I just need one more—" We don't have that. But you have to kill people's ideas because they're in love with them, but they're just not going to work, or you just don't have the resources to staff it. I think we had people like this who, if they are also forceful, they might put the organization under pressure to actually continue with the experiment beyond a reasonable point, but they are not part of the organization anymore. This is one of the personality traits that we hold dear, that it needs to fundamentally contribute to the overall target of lap time rather than what we discussed before to the department's target. There will be a decision within the group and then in the wider technical management to say, "We're stopping this now." If I was to talk to an engineer at Mercedes, I would find them surprisingly dispassionate and analytical where they're not falling in love with a particular technical approach, or they're not falling in love with their idea. It's either it works or it doesn't. Exactly. You fall in love with the result. It's that these extreme emotions and gratifications that you feel when you win a race or a championship, and therefore, the rational part of the engineer, I would hope, is superior against, let's say, an emotional falling in love with your development part. Didn't like that, and then ended up reading about internet companies in the US. I know you had Marc Andreessen here. One of the first companies that I got interested in was Netscape. I found out about all of these companies, got myself into my car, went there and said, "I like your business. I want to help you to raise some equity or list your company on the stock exchange." If it was successful with the first or the latter, I would like to have 10 or 50% of your company. That led to some shareholding because some of the people said, "Why? What remained was a skeleton of a venture capital company, very much downsized, to continue to look for opportunities whilst having the philosophical idea of this is not going to come back for a long time. I think this is an underrated aspect of good investors is knowing when to sell. I think there's an incentive in the investor ecosystem where they all like talking about, "We found this founder very early, and we bought the stock, and it grew and grew and grew, and we want to be on the journey forever." What happened is they bought a small team, Brawn, that won the championship in 2009, expecting it to be a dominant force in Formula One. It was the first Mercedes Grand Prix-owned team with its own engines and its own chassis. It underperformed. Year after year, the results were really not satisfactory. Until 2013, they won a single race only. I was at the time running Williams with the founder, and we also won a race. One of the board members approached me and invited me to Stuttgart, and he said, "How can it be that your private little team can win a race, and we, Mercedes, we can't do any better than that?" I said, "I don't know." They gave me the opportunity to look at their setup, their numbers, and everything. I came back and said, "What is your aspiration?" They said, "Winning World Championships because we bought the team that did that." I said, "Okay, then one of us is wrong because I'm working on the same budgets, and I'm thinking about finishing in the top six." They said, "No, it's not the same budget." "Look, we spend 100 million more." I said, "Yeah, those 100 millions, you spend on Michael Schumacher and Nico Rosberg, I have a pay driver from Venezuela," who won the race, by the way, Pastor. "That's the main difference." They didn't like that situation at all. I said, "I'm just a messenger, and I'm going to go back to Williams." Eventually, they came back and said, "We think our model, a corporate running a Formula One team, doesn't function, because it needs to be much more entrepreneurially led, you need to be empowered to make decisions on the spot, and we want you to run this." And I said, "I need to pinch myself because being the head of Mercedes Motorsport is probably one of the best jobs in the industry, other than being the Ferrari guy." I had to say no because I was a shareholder in Williams, and I have been always an entrepreneur. They said, "Funny that you say that because we've just bought 40% of the Abu Dhabi Sovereign Fund. We bought it back, you can buy that." I said, "I did well in my life, but I won't be able to afford it." But they helped you finance it, right? Exactly. They said, "How much can you afford?" I said, "I'm always on the conservative side, I've gone through financial hardship, and I don't want to end up there." "How much are you prepared to put into this?" I said, "That's the amount." They said, "We're going to help you finance the rest." Here I was. Until today, with the change of management now led by Ola Källenius and his colleagues, the CEO of Mercedes, they give me a framework that I can act in the best interest of the racing team outside of that corporate structure, and I couldn't wish for a better co-shareholder, partner, and brand, mighty brand that I'm associated with. The decisions that we make are quick, they are in the best interest of the team whilst thinking about the consequences for the brand, and I run it like a 20-people organization. How is how you run it different from just hired team principals, how they run teams? Every team is different. For example, when you look at Ferrari, the team principal is one of 10 direct reports to the CEO, so the marketing person in the racing team is going to be on the same level that a team principal. You have other... Very much shareholder-led organizations like Red Bull, where you would have an employed team principal that's reporting to a group of shareholders. Then you have a strictly corporate structure like Audi, where you have people from the group and from Formula One are being hired to run the team, they have a salary, they might have a bonus. That long leash that I was given. Today, there's no leash because we are partners, but the ability of taking decisions that was given to me was extraordinary because all of these people, they need to fundamentally report to someone. There's always the risk that if you take a decision without having actually escalated it through the hierarchy, you might be exposed and lose your job. Where I am, until I really badly screw up, I'm responsible for my decisions, and I'm very clear about that, but I can't be fired. That's what's interesting, right? You see this in Silicon Valley, where companies run by founders often have different strategic degrees of freedom available to them than other companies. You look at, say, with Meta, it's not a company that's just run by the shareholders, it's run by Mark, and he has a strong view that being on the forefront of AI is very important. There's been previous waves where he's had strong views, and now they're doing very well in the AI space, where maybe a professional management team would not have had the license by shareholders or the board to spend so much money on that, and it took a long time to prove out, and things like that. You see that effect oftentimes. Mercedes is the only founder-run F1 team. But it's a little bit like you and your brother around Stripe. You are able to take decisions that in a dynamic environment may prove to be false or misjudged after a few years. When you look at the ability that… The way Mark Zuckerberg leads the company is quite amazing, because you can decide to pour quite some investment in a certain field, and then say, "That hasn't developed in the way I thought." Dietrich Mateschitz, the founder of Red Bull, said something quite interesting to me one day, he said, "As an entrepreneur, you need to be like a downhill ski racer, but you need to be able to stop and walk back up the hill." I think as an entrepreneur, you can do that. You have the long-term horizon also. A manager will probably be short-term incentivized. You have a three, or you have a 5-year contract, you have stock options, you want to maximize the value of these. And there's a fear of failure. There's a fear of doing something that looks bad to the people who are grading you, because you're always being graded. Exactly. That's what I meant at the beginning. My time perspective has changed, it's 10 years, it's not one year. People know that you're a large owner of the team. I don't think quite as many people know that Jim Ratcliffe of INEOS is also a major shareholder of the team. What's he like? That was a real opportunity back in the day for Mercedes, and for Jim. He's built an empire of an organization. This is a chemicals conglomerate. There are three shareholders, they run it in the way they think it's intelligent. F1 teams have become radically more valuable. This transaction valued Mercedes F1 at 4.6 billion pounds. And that's a 7x increase from even just a few years ago. That phenomenon has also happened in other sports like NFL, and teams like that. There are two explanations you could have for the increase in value. One is a monetary theory that you have more rich people who want to buy into teams, and so the businesses are the same, but you just have more demand to become a sports team owner, and more dollars chasing fewer assets. The other is that you can say, and obviously this is somewhat true for F1, they've become better businesses. The audience has grown so much, and you have Drive to Survive, and things like that. As you think about the increase in value in Mercedes and F1 teams, how much of it is F1 teams have become a more desirable asset, and how much is it that they're actually just very good businesses now? All of this. That is going to be a longish answer. Sports teams are scarce assets today. In F1, there's just 11 of us.
