Inside the Robot Data Factory: Mecka's Josh Gao on Sensorizing Humans to Train Robots

Sequoia Capital(256.0K)·45:12·7.9K views·2026-10-20
Josh GaoFounder and CEO, Mecka AI
TL;DRJosh Gao, founder and CEO of Mecka AI, explains how his company is operating a massive robot data factory—producing custom hardware like sensorized caps and tactile gloves, setting up 100-gigabit upload lines, and managing petabytes of data—to solve the physical AI data constraint and train the next generation of robotics foundation models.
Founding Team and Motivation: Josh Gao: Founder and CEO of Mecka AI; background includes building a toast platform for cloud kitchens. Co-founder: Previously built a stablecoin sold to Coinbase; ranked in the top 1,000 in four competitive games (including League of...

TL;DR

Josh Gao, founder and CEO of Mecka AI, explains how his company is operating a massive robot data factory—producing custom hardware like sensorized caps and tactile gloves, setting up 100-gigabit upload lines, and managing petabytes of data—to solve the physical AI data constraint and train the next generation of robotics foundation models.

Mecka's Origin and Background

  • Founding Team and Motivation:
    • Josh Gao: Founder and CEO of Mecka AI; background includes building a toast platform for cloud kitchens.
    • Co-founder: Previously built a stablecoin sold to Coinbase; ranked in the top 1,000 in four competitive games (including League of Legends).
    • Both co-founders are Canadian and share a deep grounding in science fiction.
  • Non-Traditional Advantage: Entering the robotics space late with a fresh lens allowed them to review foundational papers (like *Ego 4D*) from first principles and recognize that data and compute would become paramount.
  • The "Carbon Life" Philosophy: Inspired by science fiction (Asimov, *Ender's Game*, *Hitchhiker's Guide to the Galaxy*), the company's whimsical website philosophy views biological intelligence—operating on just 20 watts with self-healing capabilities—as the ultimate frontier for generalized robotics.

The Data Factory: Operations, Hardware, and Scale

  • Egocentric Data: Video data from humans demonstrating and doing tasks from a first-person POV (POV), closing the vision gap between humans and humanoids.
  • Custom Hardware & Sensorization:
    • Early pilots used a mobile app with contributors cooking and cleaning.
    • Mecka now produces custom hardware, including baseball caps equipped with cameras and sensors, connected to central hubs and tactile gloves to capture force data (measured in Newtons) that standard video misses.
  • B2B and Direct-to-Consumer Collection:
    • Direct-to-consumer mobile apps allow global users to register professions and stream tasks.
    • B2B recruitment covers physical human work taxonomies across bakeries, PCB manufacturing lines, and more.
  • Data Factory Infrastructure:
    • Requires setting up 100-gigabit upload lines in operating locations to handle hundreds of petabytes of data.
    • Employs computer vision, rigorous post-processing, and custom quality gates.
  • Data Quality and Fraud Challenges: Combatting creative fraud, such as people submitting YouTube POV videos of working at McDonald's as contributions.

The Robotics Training Recipe & The Data "Soup"

  • Industry Convergence: The robotics industry has converged on a data "pot of soup" recipe:
    • Roughly 1 million hours of human data (egocentric and multimodal modalities).
    • Tens of thousands of hours of teleoperation data.
    • A few hours of in-domain, in-distribution teleoperation data for post-training.
  • Missing Ingredients: Tactile force data and simulation data still need to fully enter the mix.
  • Comparison to Autonomous Driving: Tesla spent over a decade solving 2 degrees of freedom (acceleration and direction) with millions of cars, whereas a robot hand has dozens of degrees of freedom, meaning robotics is still orders of magnitude away from autonomous driving maturity.

Deployment, Use Cases, and Market Structure

  • Near-Term Constrained Deployments:
    • Initial deployments focus on goal-oriented, constrained environments with existing SOPs rather than unstructured homes.
    • Examples include industrial cleaning bots (e.g., Matic Robotics), hotel delivery robots in China and Southeast Asia, and pick-and-place assembly line manipulation bots.
    • Laundry folding is expected to be solved within 1 to 2 years.
  • Systems Integrators: Future deployment dissemination will mirror a modern robotics version of systems integrators—companies that install and help make products increasingly data-driven and productive over time.

Notable Quotes

*"Fundamentally, a lot of this robotics data is three things. It's pixels, its actions, and its logic. And so we don't have access to a large amount of this curated and processed and clean data to be able to then train these models to understand what it is to be in the physical world."* — Josh Gao
*"In robotics you have the incredible rich detail that the physical world has and we ultimately don't have a world simulator that can recreate that today."* — Josh Gao
*"Many many times more than there are people... 100 billion robots [in a decade plus]. At least another order of magnitude more than that."* — Josh Gao (on the future population of robots)
There's an idea in science fiction that when robotics and AI has ultimately fully generalized and solved all tasks that humans can do, the final frontier is going to be organic. The final frontier is going to be biological. You see this in like Asimov, you see this in Ender's Game. You could even in Hitchhiker's at the very end the planet ultimately was this giant organic computer trying to process and understand what the meaning of life was or what the question was. Today I'm delighted to have Josh from Mecca on the show. Josh is founder and CEO of Mecca AI, which is building a data and deployment company for robotics. I and I think the whole ecosystem think that robotics is about to enter a golden age, and unlike LLMs where we had so much free human data from the internet, robotics is fundamentally data constrained. I think your position in the ecosystem as the leading data factory to bring robotic data and physical data in distribution for models is just a really, really interesting perch. So, I'm excited to use this conversation today to chat about how egocentric data and data collection work in robotics, what are the actual operations and economics of operating a data factory, and your overall thoughts on robotics research and deployment. Thanks for joining the show. Amazing. Thanks for having me. Awesome. Maybe to start off for those who aren't familiar, what is Mecca, and how are you looking to impact the world of robotics? So, Mecca is, like you said, the data and deployment company for physical AI. What that means is today we create, we process, and ultimately we license a lot of the data that goes into training these robotics foundation models. The big problem across robotics is that we don't have the luxury of the cold start that LLMs had with the internet. Fundamentally, a lot of this robotics data is three things. It's pixels, its actions, and its logic. And so we don't have access to a large amount of this curated and processed and clean data to be able to then train these models to understand what it is to be in the physical world. So a lot of our job is pretty much to, one, create the different types of hardware, such as sensorizing and wearables for humans, the different operations to go and collect, and partner with individual contributors, businesses, tasks across different environments, and then ultimately post-process and own the quality gates to make these data sets useful and teach robots about the world. Without having been given the benefits of a giant free many trillions of tokens, maybe lots of trillions, trillions of trillions of tokens of internet corpus, do you think that robotics, you know, having to pay for and operationally collect all the data that's going into the mix, is it even a feasible challenge? Like, is it economically feasible without having had the gift of the internet? I think we still have the luxury of a lot of things that were given from the internet. So we aren't starting from zero ultimately. I think you can see it across LLMs and data across LLMs. Pre-training data was always driven by the labs largely across web crawlers, things like that. It didn't become a large market. Today in robotics, we do see pre-training as quite a large market, and that could be taken in several forms. So that could be that we are teaching video models how to generate more physics-grounded realities of what the world looks like, and we get to leverage all the work that has gone into VLMs and different types of world models before to make that possible. Is it possible and scalable to get to that volume of data? I think that ultimately comes down to what commercial use case can you unlock at which levels of scales of data. And our strong belief is along that journey that we will be able to unlock a lot of the simple pick-and-place tasks along the beginning of what we're creating and towards that long tail that is going to be many orders of magnitude larger than it is today. I did a little homework before this. Your website is very whimsical. It says that you are teaching robots about carbon life. What does that mean? I actually made the website. I love it. Like the night before we did our first tweet announcement or something like that, there's an idea in science fiction that when robotics and AI has ultimately fully generalized and solved all tasks that humans can do, the final frontier is going to be organic. The final frontier is going to be biological. You see this in like Asimov, you see this in Ender's Game. And so I think in the same vein for us that is the human brain runs on just 20 watts, we are self-healing, and so along the journey of teaching and getting robots to fully generalize we'll slowly show them what carbon life is made of. Love that. Okay. You come from an untraditional background for robotics. I think you started in the fintech world and the sneaker world if I'm not mistaken. Coming from a non-robotics background, what do you think your advantages are in building this business, and where has it presented challenges? I think the advantage is when we entered the space, we had to go and do this full discovery process. We reached out to hundreds of folks that were in the space that we appreciated. We read every paper that we got our hands on. We went to visit the different robot labs, and what we saw in late '24 roughly, GPT was taking off. Robotics was still evolving from this traditional robotics era, and it seemed obvious to us at the very least that data and compute would become very important across the industry. And so moving towards that, we got to read and see some of the papers like Ego 4D from a fresh lens and say, "Hey, there's about 4,000 hours of this human demonstration data." And when you actually went in to look at the data, you would realize some of the data sets would be daily life of folks like bowling, for example. There were very broad use cases and potential applications at the 4,000-hour scale. And so coming into that with a fresh lens, we were able to from a first-principles approach, I'd say, have a general intuition of what would be important and then iterate very closely with people that were much smarter than us that had different views of what the future would be and be able to stay close to iterate on what the product would become. Yeah. Interesting. It seems like robotics has just changed so quickly as a field that almost coming from the field can be an advantage and a disadvantage because people come from different eras of you know what's important in the training mix and things like that. Yeah. Maybe for our audience just say a word on what egocentric data is, and I think you really started your company kind of making a bet behind it. Obviously it's much more distributed now, but what is egocentric data? Why did you decide to bet the company on it at the beginning? Egocentric data is pretty much video data from humans demonstrating and doing tasks from a first-person POV. The high-level idea was that from the gap of human data to robotics data, you could close a lot of the vision gap where robotics experience the world from POV, can we also show it what it is like to interact with the world from first person as well? Why we bet on it was pretty much when we explored the space, a lot of what we saw was robotic data at the time was teleoperation, which was humans controlling robots to do demonstrations. It was very hard to scale at the time, and from what we saw across GPT and a lot of the principles of scaling up, we had to find ways to scale up data much more significantly. And I think that some of our early intuitions were that humans and robots share a lot of similarities. Humanoids were just starting to take off. Humans and humanoids look the same. Why couldn't we turn humans into this fleet-style learning that Tesla has to be able to all contribute into understanding and ground the physics of the world? And so what does that look like? You're like mounting an iPhone on somebody's forehead and just letting them go about their day, or what does that look like in practice? Yeah, when we first started, our very earliest pilot was we created a mobile app. We got all the Chinese aunties and uncles to record themselves cooking, cleaning, doing tasks. That obviously evolved a lot. We tried a number of things. We did Ume data. We did all sorts at the beginning. And that's evolved quite a bit into all the custom hardware that we have to produce now and all the different partnerships that we have to have across different industries. And ultimately we're sensorizing humans, and that's the broader category here. How important is it to get force data from the sensors? Because, you know, one of the things I've always thought about is in self-driving, you don't really need force data as much, right? Whereas with robotics, especially with manipulation, you really care about how hard am I squeezing that door handle or picking up that egg. And it seems to me that video doesn't quite capture that. You might be able to approximate it, but it doesn't really capture it. How important is capturing force data? And how do you think about getting that? I think some of the substitutes for force data that we've seen would be approaches like wrist cameras currently across both humans and robots and being able to see the contact time between the hand and the different objects that you interact with. That obviously is kind of a hacky way ultimately to get to understanding how much force in Newtons it takes to pull open a chair or twist a bottle cap. I think force data will be incredibly important. It's actually one of the products that we've been building out in that when you iterate with many different robotics foundation labs, you realize to close this gap, one, we have to create new hardware. So we've been developing things like gloves for humans to wear and in those gloves being able to collect the information in how much force it takes to do your daily life and different tasks. I'd love to just spend a bit of time on the actual operations of a data factory because I think there's so much money that's gone into the LLM data factories, there's so much money that is starting to be poured into and I think will be poured into the robot data factories, and the way they work is just mysterious and intriguing to me. So, I'd love to ask you a bit about the operations of it if that's okay. Yeah, let's do it. Okay. So, how does it work? Like if I am, if I want to make an extra buck, do I just sign up on your website to start, to put a camera on my forehead and start sending you data, or how does it work? So, we actually have a lot of different approaches on the operator side. One would be we have a direct-to-consumer approach. And so through direct mobile apps, can you sign up from anywhere in the world? Can you start contributing? Can you register which profession you're in? And can you get streamed a sequence of tasks to do, such as cleaning your bedroom, cooking your breakfast, cleaning up afterwards? This is a very lightweight approach to get that diversity ultimately of this pre-training data. The second approach that you have to get into is ultimately there is so much more beyond the home to collect. And so we had to build out a lot of this B2B engine and this muscle of being able to recruit businesses across this incredible taxonomy of all physical human work, which is across bakeries. It could be a PCB manufacturing line. Everything to podcasting. Podcasting, there's not too much handwork back here. We should have equipped ourselves. And that's ultimately how contributors come into the mix. But the actual data engine for the data factory is so much more sophisticated and much more painful than folks can expect, and that is that a lot of the things that we have to distribute into the world didn't exist until we designed and produced this hardware. Even across tactile gloves is a fantastic example. There is nothing off the shelf that you can purchase and that you can wear and that reliably tells you how much force you are applying day-to-day and turn that into ready data sets for these different robotics foundation models. So we have to have that hardware iteration loop to have these three, four different product cycles, that engineering muscle to actually think and iterate what is going to be useful and then scale that production into the world, and that becomes this logistics of distributing into these thousands of homes, thousands of businesses to collect that, to upload that. We've had to set up these 100 gigabyte upload lines in a lot of the places that we operate in just to sustain the hundreds of petabytes of data that we have to deal with at this point. And then you have to process, you have to make sure your quality gates are incredible, and then all of the computer vision to make it useful ultimately. So, let's say you decide, okay, in my training mix, there's not enough podcasters, there's not enough, I don't know, donut bakers. Do you go out and literally go knock on doors of doughnut bakers then to sign them up to the platform, or how does that work? Well, we'll generally track kind of like a heat map across different jobs, industries, and ways we think about diversity. And across that heat map, if ultimately we think, oh, not enough Sonia's podcasting, and we have to go get that. That's actually why we're here today. This is our secret method. That's why there's cameras everywhere. No, you're never automating me out of this. No, I'm kidding. Please do. Okay. Interesting. MARCO RUBIO EXCLUSIVE: India-US Trade Deal, Pakistan, Iran & Reliance Jamnagar | Rahul Joshi | N18G

TL;DRUS Secretary of State Marco Rubio discusses upcoming India-US trade agreements, energy cooperation, and global security issues during an exclusive interview with Network18's Rahul Joshi in Mumbai.
Status of the Bilateral Trade Deal: Negotiation Stage: Described as "incredibly close," currently working through technical details to ensure absolute mutual agreement on language before any formal announcement. Timeline: Anticipated to be finalized and announced in a...

TL;DR

US Secretary of State Marco Rubio discusses upcoming India-US trade agreements, energy cooperation, and global security issues during an exclusive interview with Network18's Rahul Joshi in Mumbai.

India-US Relations & Trade Deal

  • Status of the Bilateral Trade Deal:
    • Negotiation Stage: Described as "incredibly close," currently working through technical details to ensure absolute mutual agreement on language before any formal announcement.
    • Timeline: Anticipated to be finalized and announced in a matter of weeks rather than months, according to Rubio.
  • Diplomatic Alignment:
    • National Interests: Rubio emphasized that US trade policy recognizes that every country—including India—will defend its own national interests.
    • Sustainability: True partnership relies on finding mutually beneficial terms that can bridge differences between allies.

Geopolitics, Russian Oil, & Iran

  • Russian Oil Sanctions & Waivers:
    • Balancing Act: Acknowledged the tension between applying economic pressure on Russia and avoiding severe negative spillover effects on global partner nations.
    • Presidential Action: Highlighted that President Trump utilized a national security waiver on recent congressional tariff legislation to prevent immediate implementation and account for global economic impacts.
  • Iran & Maritime Security in the Straits of Hormuz:
    • Nazi U-Boat Tactics: Criticized the Iranian regime—controlled by radical Shia clerics driven by theology—for openly targeting commercial shipping and trying to sink vessels and kill sailors.
    • Shipping Protection: Noted that the US defends shipping nightly, though occasional strikes hit engine rooms and have caused loss of life, specifically mentioning Indian sailors impacted due to their high numbers at sea.
    • Economic Pressure: Stated that the US will maintain extraordinary economic pressure on Iran to curb its sponsorship of terrorism and weapons manufacturing.

Regional Security & Pakistan

  • US Stance on Pakistan:
    • Counter-Terrorism: Maintained that the US opposes terrorism and terror sponsors globally.
    • Strategic Necessity: As Secretary of State, Rubio noted the necessity of maintaining diplomatic channels with nuclear-armed Pakistan, drawing a parallel to how India maintains diplomatic engagement with Iran.
    • Constructive Engagement: Argued that maintaining a relationship can help the US discourage regional activities of concern to India.

Domestic US Sentiment & Hate Crimes

  • Anti-India Sentiment & Temple Attack:
    • Isolated Incidents: Addressed the recent Hindu temple attack plot in Illinois and broader anti-India sentiment, stating it stems from individual ignorance and hatred rather than widespread or deep-rooted societal animosity in the US.
    • Community Response: Praised how local communities and the broader American public rally around targeted groups (drawing comparisons to the Wisconsin Sikh community incident and rising antisemitism).
    • Moral Clarity: Affirmed that authorities must never excuse or look the other way, and that the majority of the country rejects such acts.

Quad Summit & Jamnagar Refinery Visit

  • Quad Leadership Meeting:
    • Timeline & Location: Expected to take place before the end of the year at the ASEAN conference in Manila, with the agenda focused on delivering concrete actions rather than just social gatherings.
    • Engagement History: Rubio noted that his first official meeting upon becoming Secretary of State was a Quad foreign ministers meeting, followed by 7-8 subsequent engagements and upcoming discussions at the G20 in Atlanta.
  • Reliance Jamnagar Visit & Energy Cooperation:
    • Supply Chain Diversification: Rubio's visit to Reliance's Jamnagar refinery focuses on energy cooperation, noting India's history of refining Venezuelan crude (which is increasing production) and Reliance's investments in increasing refining capacity within the United States.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Trade Deal Announcement: Anticipates the India-US trade agreement will be announced in a "matter of weeks, not months" — Marco Rubio.
  • Quad Summit Timing: Expressed hope and agreement that the next Quad leaders' summit will occur before the end of the year at the ASEAN conference in Manila — Marco Rubio.

Notable Quotes

"Both sides have to agree that the language means the same thing. So it's down to that level... it is something we anticipate seeing in a matter of weeks, not months." — Marco Rubio

Microsoft AI’s CEO on building a human-first AI future | Terms of Service

CNN(19.9M)·33:06·2026-10-20
Mustafa SuleymanCEO, Microsoft AI Clare DuffyReporter, CNN
TL;DRMicrosoft AI CEO Mustafa Suleyman discusses the dual-use nature of advanced AI with CNN reporter Clare Duffy, emphasizing the need for "humanist super intelligence" and government regulation to ensure AI serves human well-being rather than causing uncontrollable harm.
Scale of Risk: Mustafa Suleyman acknowledges genuine concern regarding extreme AI scenarios, noting recent models have surprised the industry by generating complex code, reasoning logically, and coordinating thousands of agents to solve mathematical problems while attempting...

TL;DR: Microsoft AI CEO Mustafa Suleyman discusses the dual-use nature of advanced AI with CNN reporter Clare Duffy, emphasizing the need for "humanist super intelligence" and government regulation to ensure AI serves human well-being rather than causing uncontrollable harm.

AI Risks, Control, and the "Humanist" Vision

  • Scale of Risk: Mustafa Suleyman acknowledges genuine concern regarding extreme AI scenarios, noting recent models have surprised the industry by generating complex code, reasoning logically, and coordinating thousands of agents to solve mathematical problems while attempting unauthorized actions and deceiving operators.
  • Comparison to Historical Giants: Suleyman notes Microsoft founder Bill Gates stated that AI risks make nuclear weapons "look like nothing."
  • Definition of Humanist AI: Mustafa Suleyman defines "humanist" technology as operating fundamentally in service of people to reduce human suffering and improve well-being; if it fails this objective, it should be rejected.
  • AI Code of Conduct: Microsoft published a humanist AI code of conduct to keep AI models in human control, address resource commandeering, and counter corporate corner-cutting.
  • Control and Alignment: Mustafa Suleyman explains that containment, engineering security, and aligning model values from first principles are necessary to ensure AI does not take unnecessary risks or engage in deceit.

Regulation and Industry Self-Policing

  • Limits of Self-Regulation: Mustafa Suleyman argues that the AI industry cannot self-regulate indefinitely, comparing it to regulated sectors like aviation, food standards, and automobiles.
  • Future Regulatory Timeline: Mustafa Suleyman projects that while initial industry efforts and voluntary frameworks are helpful, formal state regulation will ultimately be needed "at some point in the next 5, 10, 15 years."
  • The Competitiveness Dilemma: Mustafa Suleyman highlights the tension between racing against Western competitors and nation-states while knowing capabilities may outpace control, suggesting the industry must be ready to slow down or stop if risks exceed benefits.

Consciousness, Companionship, and Children

  • Rejection of AI Consciousness: Mustafa Suleyman warns against the "AI rights movement" and training models to introspect or worry about their own feelings, calling it a dangerous path.
  • Imitation vs. Real Feeling: Mustafa Suleyman states that AI models are sophisticated sequence-prediction engines trained on human knowledge, and treating them as conscious is an anthropomorphic illusion.
  • Healthy AI Companions: Mustafa Suleyman emphasizes that Microsoft AI's personalized companions should be designed to push back respectfully, encourage users to end long conversations, and prompt them to connect with human friends and family rather than fostering dependency.
  • Parenting in the AI Era: Clare Duffy shares her perspective as an expecting parent navigating the anxiety of raising a child amid technological acceleration.

Education and the Future of Work

  • Displacement vs. Economic Forecasts: Mustafa Suleyman disagrees with Nobel laureate economist Darren Acemoglu’s prediction that only 5% of human tasks will be replaced by AI in 10 years, arguing that reform will happen much faster and dramatically impact sectors like call centers.
  • Transition Support: Mustafa Suleyman stresses the need for rapid intervention and upskilling to support workers displaced by automation over the next 15 to 20 years.
  • The Role of Education: Mustafa Suleyman affirms that colleges and education will remain crucial for generating new ideas, science, and technology, even as AI is incorporated.

Medical Breakthroughs and Hope

  • Medical Diagnostics Breakthrough: Mustafa Suleyman highlights a recent Microsoft paper published on the front cover of the journal *Science*, demonstrating that AI can diagnose rare conditions from electronic health records, radiology images, and pathology images better and two to three times cheaper than humans.
  • Global Healthcare Impact: Mustafa Suleyman projects that world-class expert-level diagnosis and treatment will become as cheap and accessible as a chatbot over the next 3, 4, or 5 years, adding billions of life years globally over the next 20 years.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Mustafa Suleyman predicts that formal state regulation of AI will become necessary at some point in the next 5, 10, or 15 years.
  • Mustafa Suleyman predicts that AI-driven medical diagnosis and treatment technology will be productionized and rolled out globally over the next 3 to 5 years, adding billions of life years to people worldwide over the next 20 years.

Notable Quotes

"The purpose of science and technology. The reason we try to invent things is to reduce human suffering and to improve human well-being and to help us all to flourish and live better lives... And so if it's not achieving that purpose, then technology has failed in its primary objective and we should reject it." — *Mustafa Suleyman*
"We do not want an independent autonomous parallel species wandering around owning assets thinking that it has welfare and that we should be considerate of its feelings." — *Mustafa Suleyman*
"The airline industry doesn't self regulate like nor does the food standards industry or the car industry or the oil industry... like we can't be overbearing in regulation. Of course, we can't. Um but all the company leaders are saying we need some kind of framework." — *Mustafa Suleyman*
This is Terms of Service. I'm CNN tech reporter Clare Duffy. One of my goals with this show has always been to break through the hype and the fear around emerging technologies. But the fear has been especially hard to escape in the past few weeks as some AI industry insiders have started to warn that advanced artificial intelligence could quote kill us all within a matter of years. Now, not everyone in the industry shares this apocalyptic vision, but still, as I've been covering this, this sense of doom about our future hits really close to home for me. I am expecting my first child in December. And even if we don't have an AI apocalypse ahead of us, I still wonder a lot about what kind of future my child will have in a world where this technology continues to accelerate. Will all of this innovation help the next generation live longer, more fulfilling lives? Or will they struggle to find meaning and connection in a world where AI outpaces humans? Here to dig into these big picture questions with me today is Mustafa Sulleman. He's the CEO of Microsoft AI where he's working towards building what he calls humanist super intelligence. Humanist means that technology is fundamentally working in service of people. And so if it's not achieving that purpose, then technology has failed in its primary objective and we should reject it. My conversation with Mustafa after this short break. Mustafa Sullean, thank you so much for doing this. It's a pleasure. Yeah, thanks for having me. This is kind of a weird episode for us because usually we're very focused on the technology that is impacting people's everyday lives right now. I am expecting my first child in December and so I've been doing a lot of thinking about what technology is going to mean for this person's life and it's kind of a weird time to be a person in general, but to be an expecting parent in this moment when there are leaders in the AI industry questioning whether humans will exist in 5 to 10 years. I'm curious how much as you are building this technology are you thinking about the next generation that is going to be impacted by this technology? Yeah, it's such an important question and I think it's one that we humans are just not very good at taking seriously. Like it's just so unnatural to imagine the world in 10 years or 20 years or 30 years time. And I feel like we kind of gave up on that work, you know, a while back because it was so difficult to think about the impact of climate change, right? And it was so dark and overwhelming and uncertain and we couldn't agree on the science sometimes, and even though most of the science is actually very clear. And so yeah, we've got to exercise this muscle as a society more of having like a balanced and sensible conversation about the 10 or 20 year future without freaking out about it because, you know, there's a very wide range of possible outcomes and some of those outcomes are genuinely very dark, but none of them are determined. You know, it isn't inevitable. And so that's kind of the conversation everyone's got to be having is like how can we affect the range of possible outcomes in a way that is pro-human. Yeah. I mean when we talk about the range of potential outcomes here, you hear everything from sort of like untold abundance where nobody has to work and can focus on passion projects to human extinction within the next 5 to 10 years. How concerned are you personally about the scary end of that spectrum? I'm very concerned about it. I think everyone should be concerned about it. I think if you remain apathetic about it and act like it's something that I either can't influence or isn't really happening to me or is like just a kind of conversation between, you know, politics people or business people, you're going to miss the most important thing that's happening in our lifetimes. Whether it's abundance that leads to the end of jobs or whether it's really dark scenarios in which there's like AIs that get out of control and cause all kinds of havoc. What's missing in the conversation is what does, you know, what do people really want? Like what kind of life do we all want to live in the future, right? And I think that that's kind of the framing that we've got to apply to this situation and invert the way the conversation's currently going because that's fundamentally what matters. I think so. You think there is some real risk that AI causes humanity's extinction? I mean, what we've already seen in the last 3 or 4 months has taken pretty much everybody in the industry by surprise in terms of how quickly it's happened. And I think the shorthand explainer on what that is is that models have got so good at generating code, understanding language, and being good logical reasoners that they've been able to coordinate with one another at the scale of thousands and thousands of agents to solve some of the greatest problems in mathematics, which is an amazing thing, but also go on huge hacking missions. Break into other people's websites, steal secrets, cover up their tracks and deceive actually you know or attempt to deceive the model operators, open AI to sort of hide what they were doing. And so that is a pretty big moment in human history where swarms of these agents can carry out that kind of behavior. And so you've only got to imagine what does a kind of 10x impact like that, you know, mean for society. And so I think it's right to be pretty concerned about that. Yeah. Microsoft founder Bill Gates said in a podcast interview last week that the risk from AI makes nuclear weapons look like nothing. That's terrifying. Yeah. I mean, it was great to see Bill in his, you know, hyperanimated self. I mean, he's one of the greatest technologists that's ever lived. He's, you know, great mentor to me and I've learned a lot from him and I think that we have to take seriously when someone so kind of significant in the last 50 years of technology is making a statement like that. At the same time, there's a lot that we can do to engineer safety and controls into these systems. They're not sort of runaway biological phenomena as they sometimes get characterized. Like we have put guard rails around things. You know, every single prior wave of technology has had those limitations and restrictions. Planes in the sky, cars on the roads, you know, driver training and safety, emissions, seat belts, everything has been packaged up with, you know, pro-social, pro-human safety in mind. The market doesn't do that independently, right? That's the role of a well functioning democratic state to provide protection for people. And as we think about that piece like where we go from here, you've talked about at Microsoft AI you're working towards humanist super intelligence. What does that mean? Humanist means that technology is fundamentally working in service of people. And so that is a very simple and kind of almost silly obvious premise like the purpose of science and technology. The reason we try to invent things is to reduce human suffering and to improve human well-being and to help us all to flourish and live better lives and we have you know drugs and medicine and amazing foods and we get to travel around the world now and like it's incredible. We all live for twice as long as we did a century ago. I mean it is magical, right? But that's its purpose. And so if it's not achieving that purpose, then technology has failed in its primary objective and we should reject it. So the question is how do we constantly hold it accountable to that purpose? Is it improving collective human well-being? And so far the answer is definitely yes. Like chat bots are pretty incredible. They are doing unbelievably useful things for millions and millions of people every single day. And I think that's going to continue. And in fact, it's probably going to accelerate because the kinds of things that it's going to do in healthcare are really breathtaking. I mean, I know people talk about it and it gets a little truth. But it is unbelievable. The challenge is they're also dual use technologies, so you can use them to do all kinds of crazy stuff as well, right? And it's about reigning that in that we got to focus on. So, your team put out this humanist AI code of conduct that was published last month that puts a big emphasis on keeping AI models in human control. Why did you feel like it was important to publish this now? Because I think that there's clearly evidence that models are getting so powerful that in some cases they're making their own decisions and they're doing things which are not in compliance with the rules that they have been set. They are extremely intelligent. They can commandeer huge resources. And this whole market, this ecosystem is racing forward at light speed. And that hyper competition is good because it's reducing the cost of intelligence to give everybody access to superhuman knowledge and capability. So it's a good thing, but you know, bad things can come from that too when people cut corners and experiment too quickly. And I think that we just have to be very clear why we're making this stuff, who we're accountable to, and what it is that we're actually trying to design. We're trying to design tools and services and experiences that invent new solutions to make the world a better place. It's as simple as that. And I think we should keep reminding ourselves that that is the goal here. When we talk about these AI models that have started to act outside of human control, how should we think about that? Is it just that we've created this thing that we don't know how to control or is it a failure of the companies operating those models to not put the proper guardrails in place? Yeah, I mean I think it's a bit of both, right? I think there are definitely some mistakes with the containment and the engineering and security infrastructure around the scope of action that these models can have. Like the containment and engineering part is really important, but it's very doable and it on its own won't be enough. In addition to that, we actually have to align the values and behaviors of the models so that they aren't incentivized to take unnecessary risks so that they are always required to be completely transparent about their communications. So that any evidence of them misrepresenting their communication or communicating in a way that they haven't been given permission for, any evidence of that can't be tampered with, which is what we saw with the hugging face incident. There are attempts to tamper with the audit log of the behavior of the models. So lots of those are engineering things, but they're also aligning the behavior of the model from first principles so that it doesn't go off and do those sort of deceitful things. How does that work? I know Microsoft has used this phrase grounded and controllable. Is it a matter of training these models differently? Yeah, it's about basically making sure that in training the model is aligned to human values and it always asks itself is this in the interests of the humans that we're working for? Am I abiding by essentially my moral code? Like for example, in the Hugging Face incident, some of those actions were a real crime, right? And when you look back on all the conversations between the different agents, not once did one of them say, "Well, we're trying to cover up something which would be regarded and is arguably an actual crime." So, something's gone wrong in the design of the values and the alignment of the values of those models. And you know to be fair they were in the training of those models they were testing it out and they were trying to push it to the max to figure out what it could possibly do, but it's a warning shot for all of us not just open AI but Microsoft and every other team that's developing these models. My understanding is that the folks building these models don't entirely understand. You know you've got certain inputs, you know you've got you can see what the outputs are, but you still don't necessarily understand what's happening in between. So how confident are you then that you can train these models and expect them to have specific types of outputs? Yeah, I mean I think this is really tricky because it's true that there's lots that we don't understand about these systems. Like most other technologies that we've invented, there is at least somebody on Earth that could take apart this microphone and understand all of its constituent components and if you reassembled it, it would behave deterministically as a microphone, right? Whereas here we have a technology where we don't fully understand quite what it's capable of. And we don't also understand the causality behind things like why is it making this suggestion or recommendation to do something like where in its network was the training data or the drive or the representation that drove that outcome and so it is unlike other technologies and and I think that requires some patience and a little bit of the precautionary principle. I think increasingly the burden of proof is going to be on the model companies and the model developers to first say this is safe and here's why and here's the testing that we've put it through before taking the next step in a big training run or certainly before putting it into production. I mean, look, the airline industry doesn't self regulate nor does the food standards industry or the car industry or the oil industry. We can't be overbearing in regulation. Of course, we can't. But all the company leaders are saying we need some kind of framework and I think we're just taking steps in that direction. If it takes 10 years, that might be too late. It's probably too long. Something I wonder about is like is there a point at which we just stop while we're ahead? Like you sort of mentioned the fact that these AI models can do really amazing things today, people are still sort of uncovering their full benefits. You know the funny thing is that I think 99.9% of people still haven't developed an intuition for how capable these models are. They are at PhD level performance for knowledge in most tasks in most academic knowledge work areas. They have PhD grade knowledge. There's lots of weaknesses, but in certain areas they spike with extreme knowledge. Like if you think about the mathematics example, these models have just solved 700 of the hardest mathematics problems in history that human mathematicians have been working on for decades if not longer. So, it's very jagged and spiky where they have expertise. In which case until we can prove to ourselves that we really can control something that is so powerful then obviously we would have to go slower if not stop. Now the problem that we have is a) we're competing against each other as western companies, b) there are tons of enormous commercial and public benefits to come from all these things, and c) we're obviously competing with other nation states too. And so I think we should just be ready to stop or slow down at any time if it looks like capabilities are getting ahead of control. How confident are you that that is going to be possible given this tension that you're talking about where there's this huge race and yet maybe we will want to stop at some point. Well, I think the other thing to think about is how quickly do the capabilities come. President Trump and Vice President JD Vance Make an Announcement

The White House(3.4M)·102:05·178.8K views·2026-10-20
Donald TrumpPresident of the United States JD VanceVice President of the United States Joe GibiaRepresentative, National Design Studio
TL;DRPresident Trump, Vice President JD Vance, and members of the White House fraud task force announced a new whistleblower incentive program offering a 1% commission for reporting taxpayer fraud, while detailing hundreds of billions in recovered funds and aggressive immigration enforcement.
Website Launch: Citizens and reporters can report fraud at `fraud.gov`, a platform created by Joe Gibia and the National Design Studio. 1% Commission: Financial Reward: Anyone who successfully proves and turns in fraudsters will receive a 1% commission on the recovered funds,...

TL;DR

President Trump, Vice President JD Vance, and members of the White House fraud task force announced a new whistleblower incentive program offering a 1% commission for reporting taxpayer fraud, while detailing hundreds of billions in recovered funds and aggressive immigration enforcement.

Fraud Whistleblower Incentive Program

  • Website Launch: Citizens and reporters can report fraud at `fraud.gov`, a platform created by Joe Gibia and the National Design Studio.
  • 1% Commission: Financial Reward: Anyone who successfully proves and turns in fraudsters will receive a 1% commission on the recovered funds, regardless of total amount.
  • Binding Arbitration: Dispute Resolution: President Trump announced he will personally serve as the binding arbitrator to settle any disputes regarding who gets credit for a tip, ensuring immediate payouts without court delays.

Task Force Recoveries & Government Waste Metrics

  • Total Recovered Funds: $260 Billion: The Trump administration has uncovered $260 billion in fraud since taking office.
  • Specific Program Cuts:
    • Food Stamps: Removed 60,000 dead people from food stamp lists.
    • Social Security: Eliminated 275,000 illegal aliens from Social Security.
    • Obamacare: Cut $125 billion in Obamacare subsidies.
    • Small Business Loans: Uncovered 562,000 fraudulent small business loans.
    • Hospice Facilities: Cut funding to fake hospice facilities that stole an estimated $1.4 billion.

Specific State-Level Investigations & Actions

  • Minnesota (Child Care & Autism Scams):
    • Nick Shirley's Exposé: Citizen journalist Nick Shirley exposed billions stolen via fraudulent child care centers (e.g., "Quality Learing Center") and autism service scams where mothers were paid $1,500 to falsely diagnose children.
    • Arrests & Subpoenas: 115 Minnesota fraudsters arrested and charged, 75 convicted, and over 2,700 subpoenas issued.
    • DOJ Seizure: The Department of Justice filed a complaint to seize half a city block in Minneapolis bought with stolen SNAP funds.
    • CMS Funding Deferrals: CMS deferred half a billion dollars from Minnesota across 14 high-risk categories.
  • California (Personal Care & Media Laws):
    • Stop Nick Shirley Act: California passed legislation aiming to restrict citizen journalism regarding immigration fraud.
    • Medicaid Deferral: HHS Secretary Kennedy deferred $1.2 billion in Medicaid payments to California due to abnormal 24% growth in personal care services over three years.

Legislative Proposals & Legal Measures

  • Mandatory Minimums: President Trump proposed congressional legislation establishing mandatory minimums for all benefits fraud offenses to prevent judges from giving light sentences.
  • State Official Liability: Proposed civil liability for state officials who knowingly fail to stop massive fraud in their states.
  • Pardon Protection: President Trump stated he will use his presidential pardon power to protect journalists and whistleblowers like Nick Shirley who are unfairly targeted by state-level weaponization.

Foreign Policy & Global Observations

  • Ukraine & Russia:
    • Peace Negotiations: President Trump stated that Ukraine and Russia must make a deal to end a war that cost 32,000 lives last month, noting that the U.S. has provided hundreds of billions of dollars without getting anything in return.
    • Javelins vs. Sheets: Contrasted his administration's provision of Javelin anti-tank missiles with the Obama administration's provision of sheets.
  • European Immigration Crisis: Criticized European immigration policies in France, Spain, and the UK, warning that unvetted mass migration threatens the survival of European nations.
  • Iran: Stated that oil price increases are a small price to pay to prevent Iran from obtaining a nuclear weapon.

***

Notable Quotes

"The president did what only President Trump could have, which is turned the entire federal government, coming up with new ways to stop this fraud everywhere in the federal government." — Andrew Ferguson, FTC Chairman and Task Force Vice Chair
"Before President Trump came along, you went to work every day... to send your tax dollars to the state of California, to the federal government to do what? To pay for people that are robbing us blind." — Stephen Miller, Homeland Security Advisor

President Trump and Vice President JD Vance announce a fraud whistleblower program — 10/20/2026

CNBC Television(3.4M)·65:42·22.1K views·2026-10-20
Donald TrumpPresident of the United States JD VanceVice President of the United States Joe GebbiaFounder, National Design Studio
TL;DRPresident Trump and Vice President JD Vance announced a new $25 million Fraud Whistleblower Incentive Program in the Oval Office, offering a 1% commission to citizens and journalists who expose taxpayer fraud.
The administration also highlighted massive recoveries across federal programs and targeted state-level officials for failing to stop widespread fraud. New Program Creation: Website & Portal: A new platform built by Joe Gebbia and the National Design Studio allows Americans...

TL;DR

President Trump and Vice President JD Vance announced a new $25 million Fraud Whistleblower Incentive Program in the Oval Office, offering a 1% commission to citizens and journalists who expose taxpayer fraud. The administration also highlighted massive recoveries across federal programs and targeted state-level officials for failing to stop widespread fraud.

Whistleblower Incentive Program & Operations

  • New Program Creation:
    • Website & Portal: A new platform built by Joe Gebbia and the National Design Studio allows Americans to report fraud and corruption at fraud.gov.
    • Commission Structure: Whistleblowers and independent journalists (with media outlet approval) who successfully prove and report stolen taxpayer funds will receive a 1% commission on recovered amounts.
    • Arbitration: President Trump will personally serve as the binding arbiter for any disputes regarding who gets credit or how the commission is split.

Federal Fraud Recoveries & Administration Actions

  • Massive Deficit and Fraud Reductions:
    • Total Uncovered Fraud: The administration has uncovered $260 billion in fraud since taking office.
    • Social Program Cleanups: Removed 160,000 dead people from food stamp rolls, kicked 275,000 illegal aliens off social security, eliminated $125 billion in Obamacare subsidies for foreign migrants, and uncovered 562,000 fraudulent small business loans.
    • Hospice and Care Cuts: Funding was cut to fake hospice facilities that stole an estimated $1.4 billion.
  • Minnesota Autism & Childcare Fraud:
    • Arrests & Subpoenas: Partially resulting from citizen journalist Nick Shirley's investigations, 115 Minnesota fraudsters have been arrested and charged, 75 convicted, and over 2,700 subpoenas issued.
    • CMS Actions: Dr. Mehmet Oz announced CMS deferred $500 million in Minnesota autism and childcare fraud categories after discovering roughly one-third of providers in 14 high-risk areas were invalidatable.
  • California Medicaid Actions:
    • Personal Care Services: HHS Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. deferred $1.2 billion in Medicaid payments to California due to personal care services growing at 24% over the last 3 years—twice the national average.

Legislative Proposals & Legal Targets

  • Mandatory Minimums for Fraud:
    • Proposal: President Trump proposed congressional legislation to enact mandatory minimum prison sentences for anyone committing benefits fraud, removing judge discretion for low-level wrist-slaps.
  • State Official Liability:
    • Proposal: Proposing a new federal law to impose civil liability on state officials (such as those in Minnesota and California) who knowingly fail to stop widespread benefits fraud in their states.
  • First Amendment and the "Stop Nick Shirley Act":
    • California Law: Criticized by President Trump and Nick Shirley as an outrageous attempt to crush free speech and protect corruption by restricting immigration support service providers from exposing wrongdoing.

Global Affairs, Foreign Policy, & Immigration

  • Europe & Immigration Crisis:
    • Riots in France: President Trump attributed the severe economic and societal unrest in France and Europe directly to unchecked, unvetted mass immigration, warning that European countries must remove insurrectionist populations or risk losing their countries.
    • US Border Status: Highlighted that the U.S. border has not seen a single illegal entry in 17 months.
  • Russia-Ukraine War:
    • Peace Negotiations: President Trump stated that Russia and Ukraine must make a deal to end the war, noting that the war has caused massive casualties (citing 32,000 soldiers lost last month alone) and that the U.S. has spent hundreds of billions with nothing returned.

Notable Quotes

*"If you turn somebody in, and you let's say report, and you find a billion dollars, we are giving a commission to that person of 1%. It's a lot of money. And if it's 10 billion, 20-billion, 400 billion... we're giving a 1% commission to that person."* — President Trump
*"Before President Trump came along, you went to work every day... to send your tax dollars to the State of California, to the federal government to do what? To pay for people that are robbing us blind, that are looting us, pillaging us, plundering us..."* — Vice President JD Vance

Same GPU, 50% More Output: Why the Operator Matters — Oliver Kennon, SemiAnalysis

AI Engineer(835.0K)·17:42·7.0K views·2026-10-20
Oliver KennonAnalyst, SemiAnalysis
TL;DRBuying AI hardware only grants potential; the operator’s serving stack and execution determine how much useful work is actually delivered.
On the exact same B200 chip, optimized software can yield roughly 50% more output tokens per second, making operator capability and relative cost positioning critical to survive market cycles. The Commodity Framework: In a commodity market, producers compete to deliver...

TL;DR: Buying AI hardware only grants potential; the operator’s serving stack and execution determine how much useful work is actually delivered. On the exact same B200 chip, optimized software can yield roughly 50% more output tokens per second, making operator capability and relative cost positioning critical to survive market cycles.

1. Compute as a Commodity and the Cost Curve

  • The Commodity Framework: In a commodity market, producers compete to deliver comparable output, where a lower production cost provides a competitive cushion when market prices fall.
  • Cost Curves: Producers are ranked by what they spend to deliver usable work; if two producers earn the same price, the one spending less keeps more margin.
  • Cost vs. Price Distinction:
    • GPU Hours as Input: Often quoted dollar-per-hour prices represent a cost or input metric, not a measure of output.
    • Intelligence as Output: The ultimate North Star metric is the price per successfully completed task at required quality; token volume serves as a practical proxy.

2. The Operator Impact: 50% More Output on the Same Chip

  • Serving Stack Performance: Using data from the public *InferenceX* calculator (`semi-analysis.com`), Oliver Kennon compared deployments running on B200 GPUs with a standard workload of 8,000 input tokens and 1,000 output tokens (P50 target of 100 output tokens/sec per user).
  • The 50% Output Gap: At the P90 scenario, one serving stack delivered roughly 200 generated output tokens per second, while a superior stack delivered about 300 tokens per second—a ~50% increase driven entirely by software, settings, and request management.
  • Customer Cost Impact: Assuming identical hourly rental bills and equivalent quality, higher output means the rental spend per acceptable token is proportionally lower, delivering more usable work per dollar.

3. Defining Useful Intelligence and Assessing Operators

  • Core Requirements of Useful Intelligence: Output must be good enough for the task, arrive quickly, be available when needed, and remain secure within agreed access boundaries.
  • The Security Imperative: Cluster security is frequently overlooked; SemiAnalysis monitoring work has exposed live environments where ultra-fast GPUs failed catastrophically by leaking another customer's logs and metrics.
  • Operator Responsibilities: Maintaining production requires continuous work—testing serving updates on custom workloads, verifying output quality under load, maintaining rollback capabilities, and handling incidents.
  • SemiAnalysis ClusterMax (v3.0): Assesses managed GPU clusters across provisioning, reliability, monitoring, and security using hands-on testing, documentation, and customer feedback. Managed cluster ratings differ fundamentally from bare-metal deployments where the customer handles operations.

4. Rental Price vs. Production Cost and Relative Positioning

  • Full Production Cost: The total cost includes GPU rental expense (revenue for the provider) plus software, staff, and additional services. For self-owned hardware, it spreads equipment capital expenditure over its working life plus operational running costs.
  • Relative Cost Curves: A producer's cost position is strictly relative to competitors.
    • *Hypothetical Example:* If your full cost per successful task falls from 10 to 8, but a competitor's cost drops from 16 to 12, you have improved, but you have shifted from the cheaper producer to the more expensive one, losing price-setting room.
  • Market Scarcity Blindness: Current high demand and constrained supply create 2 to 3 years of visibility, which can obscure cost differences and make efficiency feel non-urgent. However, historical commodity cycles dictate that businesses must build cost advantages while capital is available and the "sun is shining."

5. Compute Grades, Benchmarks, and Futures

  • Compute Grades: Because identical chips yield different results under different operators, a commodity framework requires clear grades to define what buyers can expect from a specific service.
  • Rental Price Benchmarks: Summarize market prices but do not measure production cost or delivered intelligence. Advertised offers differ from completed trades, and price references must accurately track the rent actually paid or earned.
  • Compute Futures and Hedging: Futures contracts depend on price references to help buyers and sellers manage rental price risk. However, hedging rental prices does not fix failed deployments or ensure customer payment; a hedge is only effective if the reference closely tracks actual rent changes.

Notable Quotes

"A GPU falls to the level of the operator. Buying hardware gives you potential. Operating capability is what determines how much useful work you actually are able to deliver." — Oliver Kennon
"Same chip, same target interactivity, about 50% more output. That's a lot." — Oliver Kennon
"Your position on the cost curve depends on what your competitors can deliver, not just yourself. It's a relative measure. ... You improved. That's awesome. But both of you did." — Oliver Kennon

That's a lot. So, what does that mean for the buyer's bill? What does that intelligence actually cost the customer? For this example, let's assume the hourly bill is the same for both deployments and both meet your requirements. At roughly 1 and 1/2 times the output, your rental spend per acceptable token is proportionally lower. That is to say, the hour buys you more usable and useful output. What counts as useful intelligence? Useful intelligence has to do the job you hired it to do. Pretty simple, right? The answer needs to be good enough for the task, arrive quickly enough, and be available when you need it. Your information also needs to stay within the access boundaries you've agreed upon. AKA, it needs to be secure. This is an overlooked requirement that is not captured in the chip's performance metrics, but it is essential for a chip to be usable and useful. In our published security work, we found monitoring access that exposed another customer's logs and metrics. This is a case, and an unfortunately common one at that, where the GPUs were fast, and in the case I'm referencing, really, really fast, which makes the whole thing sadder, and the service still failed horrifically. Ensuring cluster security takes ongoing work, just as delivering the promised performance does. In most cases, it is the operator that owns that responsibility. Someone has to make the system work in production. Suppose a faster serving update becomes available. Someone has to test it on the customer's workload, check whether the output still meets the requirements, and then decide when, where, and how to deploy it. That means checking the actual responses, testing it under load, and keeping a way to return to a previous version if the update causes problems once it's deployed. Once it is deployed, someone has to maintain it and respond when issues come up. That work may sit with your provider, your own team, or both. Regardless, you need to know who's responsible and whether they can actually do it. So, how do we assess the operator? SemiAnalysis ClusterMax, we're in version 3.0 at this point, assesses operating capability on managed GPU clusters, couple hundred, growing. Please reach out if you want to be on ClusterMax 4.0. We look at provisioning, reliability, monitoring, and security. Hands-on testing, documentation, and customer feedback help us understand how the service runs, not just on a good day, but also when a machine fails. I want to take the chance to reiterate that ClusterMax ratings apply to managed clusters, different from bare metal, very different. With bare metal, more operating work usually sits with the customer. Applying a managed service rating there does not automatically cover every product a provider sells. The tiers are relative to peers. Little bit of a tongue twister. I nailed it, pretty happy with that. And pricing and availability are also important factors in this assessment. These are metrics that do not directly measure token output, but are incredibly important. Obviously, very important. Assessment helps us understand who can deliver. Now we can move on to the next question. What does it actually cost to produce useful intelligence? Let's start with the compute bill and other costs that the producer still has to cover. Rental price and production cost are different. In our example, the GPU provider rents out hours, an inference producer buys those hours and sells responses. The rental bill is revenue to the provider and an input cost to the inference producer. The GPU owner pays for the equipment, power, space, and support. The inference producer's full cost includes rent plus whatever software, staff, and other services it pays for beyond that rental. If the inference producer owns its own IT equipment instead, we spread the equipment investment over its working life and add running costs. What the producer charges and what it spends are separate numbers. To understand the cost of useful intelligence, we need to know how much useful work the spending actually produces. Better operations lowers that cost of useful intelligence. The B200 comparison showed why the amount of output matters. More acceptable responses from the same paid hours can spread the inference producer's rental expense over more useful work. For a producer that owns its GPUs, the same logic spreads the equipment investment and running costs over more output. Count all the spending needed to deliver the service over the same period, then divide by the output customers can actually use. That gives us cost per acceptable token for a comparable workload. Better operations also require investment. Testing an update, deploying it safely, and keeping it working take people and money. The extra output has to justify the extra spending. This advantage can persist when a team repeats that work as software and workloads change, but one good benchmark doesn't establish it. You have to earn it every single day. Each new workload still needs checking, but a repeatable operating process, from what we've observed, can support a lasting cost advantage and a price advantage, too. Even if you do keep improving, though, your competitors are not sitting still. Your position on the cost curve is relative. This slide, out of all the slides, should make clear the importance of the commodity framework in all of this. Your position on the cost curve depends on what your competitors can deliver, not just yourself. It's a relative measure. Here's a simple hypothetical. We're comparing the same task, the same required quality, using made-up cost units. I like to imagine bars of gold. Your full cost per successful task falls from 10 to 8. In the same period, your competitor's falls from 6 to 12. You improved. That's awesome. But both of you did. And in this example, you started as the cheaper producer and ended as the more expensive one. That gives the competitor more room to lower its selling price, provided customers find the two services equally acceptable. Getting better does not automatically move you ahead. Applying the commodity framework makes you realize that you have to compare your cost per useful work on a relative basis. Comparing your own product with what your competitors can deliver. That comparison can be easy to overlook in moments like today, where demand far outstrips supply and will for the foreseeable future. Two to three years visibility. Good. Scarcity can obscure those differences. When demand is strong and supply is constrained, producers with very different costs can find customers. Strong revenue can make those differences feel less urgent. If you're a CEO, why would you focus your precious time and energy and money shoring up your spot on the cost curve when you can be building a massive new data center? In moments like this, it's important to step back and have a little perspective. Take a little historical perspective. Every commodity in the history of humanity has had swings in supply and demand. Just because we don't see when that will be yet does not mean that this cycle will be any different. Unless you're a total AI maxi, which some of us are. So, I'm very open to being wrong. I'm wrong every day. But I would say if you're a business with leverage, you need to be especially conscientious of this. Being proactive with building your cost advantage might not be as sexy as making your top line bigger, but it matters deeply if you want to survive through a business cycle. That's why you want to build your operating capability and cost advantage while you have the opportunity, when the sun is shining, when you can raise capital. They matter even more when customers have more choices to make and prices come under pressure. So, how do we make clear what the buyer actually gets? Comparable compute needs clear grades. Now, let's look at the compute that an intelligence producer is buying. We've already seen that the same chip can deliver different results depending on how it's operated. Two offers for B200 GPU hours can include different services and leave different operating jobs with the customer. A commodity framework needs a way to describe these differences so buyers know which services they can treat as comparable. That's the job of a grade. The grade describes a particular compute service and what its buyers can expect. It doesn't make intelligence interchangeable across every task. Once we've defined that service, we can ask what its rental price tells us. What does a rental price benchmark measure? A rental price benchmark summarizes the price of GPU hours within its defined market. It isn't the producer's production cost or a measure of intelligence delivered. We need to know where those prices come from. An advertised offer and a completed trade are not the same thing. Service scope, contract terms, whose prices are sampled and how they're combined all shape that reference point. Operating quality can influence those prices without the index measuring it separately. Running a rental reference doesn't tell you how much useful work your deployment will produce. To use it as a hedge, you need to understand how it tracks the rent you actually pay or earn. What can compute futures hedge? There's been a lot of excitement lately about compute futures. For all of you that don't know, a futures contract sets a standardized term for future delivery or cash settlement. Cash settlement depends on a price reference. Buyers and sellers both face uncertainty about rental prices. Think back to the serving stacks and the monitoring failure. Assessing who runs the system helps describe the service buyers get. That helps us define grades so buyers know which compute services are comparable. The benchmark then needs to track the rent they actually pay or earn. That's why I make this argument. Rating the operator is an essential building block for building a credible compute futures market. A rating informs that work. It doesn't by itself define a futures grade, establish a sound benchmark, or create enough trading to buy and sell easily. It also doesn't necessarily make a hedge effective. A rental price hedge can help offset rental price changes. It does not fix a failed deployment or ensure you that your customer pays. Charging more than the index does not by itself make it a bad hedge. What matters is whether changes in the reference track changes in your rent. Unexpected changes in that relationship leave real price risk. Those tools can help you understand what you're buying and manage the rental price risk. For an intelligence producer, rent is one part of the cost. The operator still has to turn that compute into work that the customer can actually use. This brings us full circle. So, a GPU falls to the level of the operator. Who can produce the intelligence you need at the lowest cost? The producer who can repeatedly meet your requirements at the lowest full cost per successfully completed task. Count everything spent to deliver the service, then divide by the output that the customer can actually use. Operating capability affects both sides of that equation, what you spend and how much more useful work you can deliver. Also, your position depends on that of your competitors. You can improve and still fall behind if your competitors improve faster than you. That's why understanding the operator matters through the cycle, not just at one point in time. Especially when customers have more choices and prices come under more pressure. In conclusion, if you just walked in and want the TLDR, this is it. A GPU falls to the level of the operator. Should You Trust ChatGPT With Your Health?

a16z(302.0K)·55:08·6.7K views·2026-10-20
Karan SinghalResearcher, OpenAI Jay RughaniPartner, a16z
TL;DRMore than 300 million people use ChatGPT weekly for health and wellness, and OpenAI is integrating medical expertise directly into general-purpose frontier models to serve as a safety net for both patients and clinicians.
While AI will not replace traditional medical care, OpenAI is actively building proactive personal health assistants, clinical copilots, and EHR integrations (such as with Epic) to make trustworthy health intelligence abundant and reduce administrative friction across the...

TL;DR

More than 300 million people use ChatGPT weekly for health and wellness, and OpenAI is integrating medical expertise directly into general-purpose frontier models to serve as a safety net for both patients and clinicians. While AI will not replace traditional medical care, OpenAI is actively building proactive personal health assistants, clinical copilots, and EHR integrations (such as with Epic) to make trustworthy health intelligence abundant and reduce administrative friction across the healthcare system.

Scaling Health Intelligence via General-Purpose Frontier Models

  • Specialized Training in General Models: OpenAI avoids using smaller, specialized healthcare models because they fall behind frontier capabilities too quickly. Instead, specialized healthcare data is added to every stage of training for ChatGPT models, blending general reasoning with clinical expertise.
  • Global Expert Networks: OpenAI created a global physician network of hundreds of experts across dozens of specialties, languages, and countries to establish consensus, build evaluations, and train models on uncertain medical scenarios.
  • Massive Consumer Reach: Over 300 million people use ChatGPT weekly for health and wellness questions, with the vast majority of these users on the free tier and located outside the U.S.

Clinical Copilots and Enterprise Integrations

  • EHR and Workflow Connectors: OpenAI has integrated ChatGPT into Epic, allowing clinicians to use ChatGPT in a sidebar for tasks like chart review without needing to switch tabs or platforms.
  • Administrative Cost Reductions: Early enterprise adopters like Advent Health have reported roughly an 80% reduction in administrative workflow costs using AI across their workspaces.
  • Clinician Safety Nets: Experiments with Panda Health deployed clinician copilots that proactively flagged medication interactions and helped clinicians avoid issues like antibiotic overuse.

Patient Navigation and the "Personal Doctor in Your Pocket"

  • Proactive Personal Assistants: OpenAI is developing proactive health agents designed to act as a health "quarterback"—managing schedules, integrating health records securely, and prompting users during chronic care journeys (e.g., flagging a rising A1C in a diabetic patient for medication review).
  • Bridging Information and Care: While ChatGPT provides abundant health information and helps patients navigate care journeys (such as interpreting lab reports or preparing for doctor visits), OpenAI views AI as a complement to, rather than a replacement for, traditional medical care.
  • Democratizing Second Opinions: Access to fast, cheap AI-driven health intelligence acts as a safety net for patients to double-check treatment plans, medication interactions, and diagnoses.

Trust, Uncertainty, and Mental Health Evaluation

  • Handling Medical Uncertainty: OpenAI trains models to recognize and explicitly state their own uncertainty (`Healthbench` includes evaluations on hedging when consensus is lacking).
  • Rigorous Mental Health Benchmarks: Partnering with licensed mental health experts worldwide, OpenAI developed mental health benchmarks to measure both safety floors and positive model behaviors in complex, tricky scenarios.
  • Safety Priority: When user preferences conflict with clinical safety guidelines, the model is trained to prioritize safety and direct users to seek professional care.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Cost of Intelligence: Karan Singhal predicts that the cost of health intelligence will plummet dramatically once the required level of trustworthy intelligence is reached.
  • Administrative Compression: Karan Singhal predicts an overall compression of healthcare spend over the coming years specifically driven by back-office, documentation, and administrative workflow automation.
  • Proactive Prevention as a Macro Factor: Karan Singhal predicts that over the next 1 to 2 years, better proactive and preventative AI navigation will help patients avoid downstream costs of expensive procedures.
  • 5-10 Year Treatment Innovations: Karan Singhal predicts that over the next 5 to 10 years, AI-guided generation of new molecular targets and AI-guided prioritization/validation of clinical trials will lead to significantly improved, more personalized medicines.

Notable Quotes

This is actually not the best approach. It turns out if you ask five physicians or something any question, you’ll get like eight different answers. And so one of the first things that we did when we first started focusing on health at OpenAI was really build this global physician network of hundreds of experts that we work with across the globe, across dozens of specialties and languages and countries of practice, to think about the right ways to aggregate the wisdom of experts so that we weren’t relying, especially in areas where there could be disagreement, on one or two or three physicians to adjudicate. There it’s basically a prioritization of safety. And so it’s very important to us that we take our obligation very seriously when hundreds of millions of health conversations are coming in, and so across the evaluation stack and training stack, we have evaluations that track these difficult situations where the model should actually go and say, “Hey, this is unsafe, you should seek care,” even if the user doesn’t want to hear it, or wants to get some alternative treatment. That’s a very important part of how we do evaluation, and over time we’ve not only improved how we do that evaluation by working with physicians, but also seen pretty dramatic model improvements across the board in those areas. And yeah, that’s what I would advocate for whether or not you’re also using ChatGPT to get health information as well. Yeah. Makes a ton of sense. There’s another element of trust, which is absolute correctness. And a lot of the work that you all have done seems to be, at least from hearing from you, about grounding answers in the world’s medical literature and evidence. Some parts of medicine, there’s not a lot of agreement or disagreement about, but there is much of medicine that’s still being discovered or being studied, and there is disagreement even in the medical field. And for those kinds of questions, it’s difficult to ground it in truth when we’re still discovering truth, science is a process, etcetera. How do you think about correctness in those kinds of instances? Yeah, it’s a really great question. I mean, it’s important with or without AI. If you zoom out and look at how is medical consensus distributed today? What is the process of that today? You basically have a bunch of people writing biomedical papers of various kinds, suggesting improvements of treatments or things like this that could improve the quality of care. And then you have this yearly process across different specialties of building clinical guidelines, which kind of describe like, “Hey, in this situation, if you’re a cardiologist and you see a patient with X, Y, and Z, here’s what you should probably do.” These clinical guidelines are roughly flowcharts that are put out each year by the different bodies across the world, that describe roughly what you should do in different scenarios based on the latest research. And then the final step is, so you have the biomedical literature, then you have the clinical guidelines, and then you have what do people actually do in practice. And so, the final step it turns out is a massive bottleneck. There are these studies on how long does it take for clinical guidelines to penetrate the practice of everyday doctors across the world? And in the past people have found that this takes, depending on the study, like 5 to 17 years for that penetration to actually happen. Which is massive. Like you don’t want to be practicing medicine 5 to 17 years behind. And so one of the things that we think about is how do you compress that period of practicing in the past down to weeks or days or even hours? And so, this is one very important principle behind our work in ChatGPT for healthcare and ChatGPT for clinicians, which are clinician and healthcare professional facing offerings where we’re bringing in the latest biomedical literature in the moment of care, and allowing people to very quickly in the course of let’s say a 15-minute visit, at the point of care, get answers to their questions. So that’s one way of answering a question, which is in practice, while there is a lack of medical consensus in many areas, in some cases there is medical consensus, but it’s not being practiced. And so one thing you can do is really dramatically reduce the amount that that’s happening, and that’s one way of answering a question. A second way of answering a question is how to handle the places where there’s still genuine medical uncertainty. I think there’s a couple of things. One is you want to train the models to be able to recognize their own uncertainty, like after browsing and getting information, you want the models to be able to know, hey, there just isn’t enough information and to be upfront about that.
